OSSLT Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Passage real_life_narrative_1

For three weeks, I practiced the same four bars of the song until my fingers could find the chords without me even looking at the guitar. My little brother Aiden always used to like when I performed, laughing so hard I couldn't sing. But this was different: this was for Cedarbrook's Winter Talent Show, in front of the whole school, not just my family in the living room.

I signed up in October, back when the idea of standing on the gymnasium stage felt exciting instead of terrifying. My best friend Simone convinced me to do it together. She would sing while I played guitar. We picked an old folk song our grandmothers used to hum, something slow and warm that would sound good even if my hands shook a little.

By December, my hands were shaking a lot. Every night before bed, I imagined forgetting the chords in front of two hundred students. I imagined my voice cracking on the high note in the second verse. I told Simone I wanted to drop out three separate times. Each time, she reminded me that Ms. Alvarez, our music teacher, had said something on the first day of class that I kept coming back to: everyone in that room is nervous about something, and courage is not the absence of fear, it is doing the thing anyway.

The night of the show, I stood backstage watching a boy juggle tennis balls and a group of Grade 9 students perform a dance routine that got the whole gym clapping along. My stomach felt like it was full of moths instead of butterflies. When our names were called, Simone squeezed my hand and whispered that we only needed to get through the first line, and after that, our bodies would remember the rest.

She was right. The first chord came out steady, and once I heard my own sound fill the gymnasium, something in my chest loosened. I stopped thinking about the audience and started listening to Simone's voice blending with the guitar. When we finished, the applause felt less like noise and more like proof that I had actually done it.

Afterward, my music teacher found me near the drink table and told me she could see how much I had grown since September, when I used to ask to play with my back turned to the class. She said that kind of growth does not happen by accident, it happens because someone decides to keep showing up even when it is uncomfortable.

Walking home that night with Aiden, who had come to watch, I realized the version of me from October would not have believed I could feel this calm standing under bright lights in front of the whole school. I still get nervous before I perform. I just do not let that nervousness make the decision for me anymore.

  1. The correct answer is B (that they only needed to get through the first line and their bodies would remember the rest): the passage states directly that Simone squeezed the narrator's hand backstage and whispered this exact reassurance just before their names were called. Option A describes a vague comfort that never actually appears in the text. Options C and D invent details about grades and skipping the verse that are never mentioned anywhere in the passage.
  2. The correct answer is D (her fear of forgetting the chords or cracking on the high note felt overwhelming): the passage explains that she imagined forgetting chords and cracking on the high note every night before bed, which directly explains her repeated urge to quit. Option A is never suggested since she chose that song herself with Simone. Options B and C describe conflicts that are never mentioned anywhere in the passage.
  3. The correct answer is A (standing at the free throw line during a tied game with everyone watching): both situations involve intense public pressure, physical nervousness, and the fear of failing in front of a crowd at a single decisive moment. Option B lacks any audience or pressure at all. Options C and D describe calm, low stakes activities that do not carry the same nervous, high visibility feeling the narrator describes.
  4. The correct answer is C (the tight feeling of nervousness she had been carrying started to ease and relax): the passage describes her stomach full of nerves backstage, and once the first chord came out steady, that tension visibly began to fade. Option A misreads the word as pain rather than relief. Options B and D describe events the passage never mentions happening during the performance.
  5. The correct answer is B (October, when the idea of performing still felt exciting rather than frightening): the passage states directly that she signed up in October, back when the idea still felt exciting instead of terrifying. Option A refers only to when she began asking to play with her back turned, not to signing up. Options C and D describe later points in the story, after the sign up had already happened.
  6. The correct answer is D (facing something frightening and continuing anyway can build real confidence over time): the passage ends with the narrator noting she still gets nervous but no longer lets that nervousness decide for her, echoing Ms. Alvarez's earlier comment about courage. Option A contradicts the narrator's own growth from a nervous beginner into a calmer performer. Options B and C describe ideas the passage never actually supports or suggests.
  7. The correct answer is A (to show how much her confidence had grown between September and the night of the show): Ms. Alvarez directly points out this contrast, and the detail highlights just how far the narrator had come by December. Option B contradicts the supportive, encouraging tone Ms. Alvarez uses throughout the scene. Options C and D introduce ideas about strategy and comparison that the passage never actually makes.
Passage real_life_narrative_8

My aunt's bakery on Elm Street had always smelled like cinnamon and burnt sugar, but until this summer I had only ever visited it as a customer. When she offered me a part time job scooping icing and boxing muffins for minimum wage, I said yes mostly because I wanted enough money to fix my bike before September.

The first week taught me that running a bakery is nothing like eating at one. Aunt Rosa arrived before sunrise to start the ovens, and by the time I showed up at seven, the whole shop already smelled like fresh bread. My job seemed simple: box orders, wipe counters, and restock the display case. It turned out I was terrible at all three. I dropped a tray of cinnamon rolls on my second day, mislabelled a birthday cake with the wrong name on my fourth, and completely forgot to restock the croissants before the Saturday morning rush.

Aunt Rosa never raised her voice about any of it, which somehow made me feel worse than if she had. Instead, after the croissant mistake, she sat me down during a quiet moment and explained that customers who come in on Saturday mornings often plan their entire weekend around picking up something from her shop. If the croissants are gone, she said, it is not just an empty shelf, it is a small disappointment in someone's morning. She told me that paying attention to small details is what keeps people coming back.

I did not fully understand what she meant until the following week, when a regular customer named Mr. Chen came in looking for the same seeded rye loaf he bought every Thursday. I had restocked it that morning simply because I remembered him mentioning it once. When I handed it to him before he even asked, his whole face lit up. He told me it was the highlight of his week. That small moment stuck with me more than any lecture could have.

By August, I had stopped thinking of the job as just a way to earn money for bike parts. I started noticing which customers liked their coffee black, which ones always split a treat with their kids, and which display trays needed refilling before the lunch crowd arrived. Aunt Rosa noticed too. She started trusting me to open the shop alone on Sundays, which felt like a bigger deal than any paycheque.

When I finally bought the parts to fix my bike near the end of the summer, I realized the job had given me something else I had not expected. I understood now that showing up on time and doing small tasks carefully was not just about avoiding mistakes, it was about respecting the people who depended on you, even in a job that seemed as ordinary as boxing muffins.

  1. The correct answer is C (he put the wrong name on a birthday cake that a customer had ordered): the passage states directly that he mislabelled a birthday cake with the wrong name on his fourth day. Option A describes the mistake from his second day, not his fourth. Options B and D describe separate events that happened at different points in the story, not on day four.
  2. The correct answer is A (she wanted him to genuinely understand why the mistake mattered rather than just feel scolded): her explanation about customers planning their weekends around the shop shows she cared about him understanding the impact, not simply feeling punished. Option B contradicts her continued patience and later trust in him. Options C and D are contradicted directly, since she made time to sit with him and she specifically described the disappointment an empty shelf would cause.
  3. The correct answer is D (arriving at a favourite restaurant only to learn your usual order is unavailable): this mirrors the small, specific disappointment Aunt Rosa describes a regular customer feeling when a familiar item is missing. Option A involves no comparable disappointment about a small routine. Options B and C describe personal, low stakes situations that do not involve another person's expectations being let down.
  4. The correct answer is B (it encourages customers to return to the bakery again and again over time): the surrounding context is entirely about customer habits, like Mr. Chen returning weekly, which directly supports this meaning. Option A confuses employees with customers, who are not discussed in that sentence. Options C and D introduce unrelated ideas about equipment and expansion that the passage never mentions.
  5. The correct answer is C (open the shop by himself without any supervision on Sunday mornings): the passage states directly that Aunt Rosa began trusting him to open the shop alone on Sundays by August. Option A is never mentioned as something he was given responsibility for. Options B and D describe responsibilities far beyond what the passage actually describes him taking on.
  6. The correct answer is A (careful, small everyday actions can genuinely matter to the people who depend on them): the closing paragraph states this directly, connecting small tasks like boxing muffins to respecting the people who rely on him. Option B is never discussed or supported anywhere in the passage. Options C and D are contradicted, since the narrator ends up valuing the job for more than money and clearly sees that customers do notice small details.
  7. The correct answer is D (to highlight the moment he began to understand the real value of paying attention): the narrator states this moment stuck with him more than any lecture could, marking a turning point in his understanding. Option A is never suggested anywhere in the passage. Options B and C overstate or misrepresent what the passage actually shows about Mr. Chen and the narrator's priorities.
Passage real_life_narrative_15

Last spring, I was the last player cut from the Riverside United soccer team, standing in the parking lot holding a duffel bag I had packed with new cleats I never got to wear in a single game. Coach Whitfield told me my footwork needed work and that I should keep practicing over the summer. I nodded and said thank you, even though what I really wanted to do was quit soccer completely.

Instead, I spent most evenings that summer at the field behind my apartment building, dribbling a worn out ball around a row of orange cones my dad helped me set up. My younger cousin Riley, who was only nine, would sometimes join me and pretend to be a defender, laughing every time I tripped over my own feet. Some nights I felt like I was getting nowhere. My left foot still felt clumsy compared to my right, and every time I imagined tryouts again, my stomach tightened.

By July, I found a video online of a drill that focused only on using the weaker foot for short, controlled passes. I practiced it almost every day, even when it felt repetitive and boring. My dad noticed and started timing me with his phone, cheering whenever I finished a set of passes faster than the week before. Slowly, without me really noticing at first, my left foot stopped feeling like a stranger attached to my leg.

When tryouts came around again in August, I recognized several of the same players from the year before, including a few who had made the team while I had not. This time, though, something felt different. When the coach called for a passing drill using both feet, I did not hesitate the way I had the previous year. My legs simply did what months of practice had taught them to do.

I still remember the exact moment my name was read off the final roster taped to the gym door. I read it three times just to be sure. Riley, who had come with me that day, jumped up and down shouting that she basically deserved a spot on the team too since she had trained me all summer.

Looking back, I do not think making the team the second time around felt as exciting as I expected it to feel a year earlier. It felt more like relief, and something quieter than excitement, a kind of proof that the boring, repetitive evenings with the cones actually meant something. Coach Whitfield told me afterward that she remembered cutting me the year before, and that watching my improvement over one summer was exactly why she still believed tryouts should happen every single year.

  1. The correct answer is B (that her footwork needed improvement and she should keep practicing over the summer): the passage states this feedback directly in the opening paragraph as the exact reason Coach Whitfield gave. Option A is never mentioned anywhere in the passage. Options C and D invent conditions and reasons that the passage never actually states.
  2. The correct answer is D (he wanted to encourage her by showing how much her weaker foot was improving): the passage says he cheered whenever she finished a set faster than the week before, showing his intent was encouragement through visible progress. Option A is never suggested or supported anywhere in the passage. Options B and C describe motivations the passage does not actually provide any evidence for.
  3. The correct answer is A (checking a results list after months of practicing for something that matters deeply): both situations involve nervous anticipation followed by a meaningful, personal payoff after sustained effort. Option B describes a routine task with no emotional weight attached to it. Options C and D describe predictable, low stakes activities that do not carry any comparable anticipation or personal investment.
  4. The correct answer is C (her weaker foot had become far more comfortable and familiar to control): this follows directly from the months of drills focused on her weaker foot, which the passage describes right before this sentence. Option A introduces an injury that is never mentioned anywhere in the passage. Options B and D contradict the passage's focus on training her weaker foot, not replacing it or getting new gear.
  5. The correct answer is D (by July, after weeks of practicing at the field near her apartment building): the passage states directly that she found this video by July, following earlier summer practice sessions. Option A contradicts the passage, since she had already been practicing for weeks before finding the video. Options B and C describe timing that does not match the sequence of events the passage actually lays out.
  6. The correct answer is B (repetitive, unglamorous practice can lead to real progress even when results feel slow): the passage repeatedly describes boring, repetitive drills that eventually produce real improvement, which is confirmed when Talia makes the team. Option A directly contradicts the passage's clear focus on effort over natural ability. Options C and D are never suggested and are also contradicted, since Riley is described as genuinely helpful during practice.
  7. The correct answer is C (to show how much the difficult, quiet work over the summer had changed her): the passage explicitly connects this quieter feeling to proof that the repetitive practice had genuinely meant something. Option A contradicts her continued dedication to soccer throughout the story. Options B and D introduce ideas about blame and credit that the passage never actually raises.
Passage real_life_narrative_22

Nadia and I had been best friends since Grade 6, so when our English teacher assigned us to the same group for the final project on Ontario history, I assumed it would be the easiest partnership of the semester. I was wrong almost immediately. Nadia wanted to build the entire presentation around interviews with our grandparents, while I wanted to focus on a timeline poster with dates and images, something I thought would be simpler to finish on time.

We argued about it quietly at first, the kind of disagreement where neither person actually says they are annoyed. Nadia started replying to my texts more slowly than usual. I stopped mentioning the project altogether whenever we hung out after school, partly because I did not want to start another argument, and partly because I was still frustrated that she had dismissed my timeline idea without really considering it.

Two weeks before the deadline, we had barely started any actual work. During a group meeting in the library, our third teammate, Felix, finally said out loud what both of us had probably been thinking: he did not care which idea we used, he just wanted us to stop being annoyed with each other long enough to actually finish something. Nadia looked at me and admitted, quietly, that she had been avoiding the conversation because she was scared that disagreeing about a school project might turn into something bigger between us.

That admission surprised me. I had assumed she just thought my idea was boring, not that she was worried about our friendship. I told her honestly that I felt the same way, and that avoiding her texts had felt worse than any argument about a poster could have felt. Once we actually said that out loud, combining our two ideas suddenly seemed obvious. We built a timeline poster that included short quotes pulled directly from interviews with both of our grandparents, giving the historical dates a personal, human element neither version alone would have had.

We finished the project two days before it was due, staying up late at Nadia's kitchen table cutting out photographs and taping quotes beside the correct years. Ms. Dufresne gave us one of the highest marks in the class, but honestly, the mark mattered less to me than realizing how close Nadia and I had come to letting a disagreement about a poster damage something we had built over four years.

Now, whenever we start a new project together, we agree on one rule before anything else: if either of us feels frustrated, we say so immediately instead of letting it sit quietly underneath everything else we do.

  1. The correct answer is A (building the presentation around interviews with their own grandparents): the passage states directly that Nadia wanted to build the entire presentation around interviews with their grandparents. Option B describes a format that is never mentioned as one of Nadia's ideas. Options C and D introduce project formats that the passage never actually discusses.
  2. The correct answer is C (he wanted to avoid starting another argument about their disagreement on ideas): the passage states this reason directly, alongside his lingering frustration about the timeline idea being dismissed. Option A is never suggested anywhere in the passage. Options B and D describe assumptions that the passage gives no evidence for at all.
  3. The correct answer is B (avoiding a conversation with a close friend after a quiet disagreement): this closely mirrors how Owen and Nadia avoided discussing their disagreement directly, letting tension build quietly instead. Option A involves a low stakes decision with no emotional tension involved. Options C and D describe ordinary situations that do not involve any strained relationship or avoidance.
  4. The correct answer is D (they privately shared a similar feeling that neither had yet admitted aloud): this is confirmed moments later, when Nadia admits she had been avoiding the same conversation Owen had also been avoiding. Option A contradicts the passage, since both were clearly aware the project was behind schedule. Options B and C are contradicted by the fact that Felix's comment is what actually triggers their honest conversation.
  5. The correct answer is A (two days before the project was due, after staying up late together): the passage states directly that they finished the project two days before it was due, staying up late at Nadia's kitchen table. Option B describes an earlier point in the story, before the actual work was finished. Options C and D describe timing that does not match the sequence of events the passage lays out.
  6. The correct answer is C (speaking honestly about frustration early can protect a friendship from lasting damage): the closing paragraphs describe the new rule Owen and Nadia adopt, which is built entirely around this idea. Option A is never suggested and is contradicted by their strong four year friendship. Options B and D are not supported anywhere, since combining both ideas is what actually worked and no teacher intervention is mentioned.
  7. The correct answer is B (to show how the experience changed the way they now handle disagreements together): the rule directly reflects the lesson they learned about speaking up early, tying the ending back to the whole conflict. Option A is never suggested anywhere in the passage. Options C and D introduce ideas about Felix and documentaries that the passage never actually raises.
Passage real_life_narrative_29

The first time I tried to drop into the shallow bowl at the Northside skatepark, I chickened out so badly that I sat down on the edge and just watched other kids glide past for almost twenty minutes. I had gotten the skateboard for my birthday in June, and by August I could barely ride in a straight line without wobbling, let alone attempt anything that involved a ramp.

My neighbour Deshawn, who was two years older and had been skating since he was seven, noticed me sitting there for the third weekend in a row. Instead of laughing, which is honestly what I expected, he sat down next to me and asked what exactly I was afraid of. I told him it was not really the height of the bowl, it was the sound other skaters made when they fell, that hard slapping noise against the concrete that made my stomach drop just from hearing it.

Deshawn told me something that stuck with me for the rest of the summer. He said almost every skater who looked confident had fallen hundreds of times before they looked that way, and that the sound of falling only feels scary from the outside, not from the inside, once you learn how to fall correctly instead of just stiffening up. He spent the next hour teaching me how to roll out of a fall on flat ground, tucking my shoulder and letting my body absorb the impact instead of catching myself with my wrists.

The following weekend, I practiced falling on purpose, over and over, on a gentle slope near the entrance of the park, until it stopped feeling like an emergency and started feeling almost routine. Only after that did I let myself try dropping into the shallow end of the bowl for real. My first attempt was clumsy and I did fall, but because I already knew how to roll, it barely hurt at all.

By the end of August, I could drop into the bowl and ride it out cleanly most of the time, though I still fell occasionally, usually when I overthought it instead of just trusting my body. Deshawn started calling me over whenever a newer kid showed up looking nervous, the same way he had once done for me. I found myself repeating almost exactly what he had told me, that the scariest part of skating is rarely the fall itself, it is usually just the sound of it from far away.

Looking back, I think what changed that summer was not really my skating ability, even though that improved too. What changed was realizing that fear often exaggerates things that are far more manageable up close than they seem from the sidelines.

  1. The correct answer is D (the hard slapping sound other skaters made against the concrete when they fell): the passage states this directly, with Jake explaining that it was the sound of falling, not the height, that scared him. Option A is explicitly ruled out, since Jake says it was not really the height of the bowl. Options B and C describe fears that the passage never actually mentions.
  2. The correct answer is B (he believed learning to fall safely would make the actual bowl feel less frightening): this fits directly with his earlier explanation that falling only feels scary until you learn how to do it correctly. Option A contradicts the outcome, since Jake later does successfully attempt the bowl. Options C and D introduce ideas about staff rules and equipment that the passage never mentions.
  3. The correct answer is A (standing at the top of a diving board, watching others jump before working up courage): both situations involve watching others succeed at something intimidating while hesitating to attempt it yourself. Option B is a calm, familiar activity with no fear involved at all. Options C and D describe ordinary, low pressure situations that do not involve comparable hesitation or fear.
  4. The correct answer is C (an ordinary, familiar action that no longer felt alarming or unusual to him): this follows directly from Jake practicing falling on purpose until it stopped feeling like an emergency. Option A misreads the word as still meaning something dangerous, which contradicts the sentence. Options B and D introduce ideas about stopping practice and required rules that the passage never actually mentions.
  5. The correct answer is D (he practiced falling on purpose on a gentle slope near the park entrance): the passage states this directly as what he did the following weekend, before attempting the bowl itself. Option A contradicts his continued dedication to skateboarding throughout the passage. Options B and C describe events that happen later in the story or never happen at all.
  6. The correct answer is A (fear can often make a challenge seem far worse than it actually feels up close): the final paragraph states this idea directly, describing how fear exaggerates things that are more manageable up close. Option B is contradicted by Jake's successful progress throughout the passage. Options C and D are directly contradicted, since Deshawn helps him repeatedly and Jake continues to fall occasionally even after improving.
  7. The correct answer is B (to show how much his own understanding and confidence had grown since June): passing along Deshawn's advice shows Jake has fully absorbed the lesson and can now offer it to others himself. Option A is never suggested anywhere in the passage. Options C and D introduce ideas about park rules and tricks that the passage never actually raises.
Passage real_life_narrative_36

When my mom suggested I volunteer at the Maplewood Animal Shelter over winter break instead of spending the entire two weeks watching videos in my room, I agreed mostly because I was bored, not because I particularly loved animals. I had never owned a pet, since our apartment building did not allow them, and I assumed volunteering would mostly involve cute, friendly dogs that wanted to be petted constantly.

Most of the dogs at the shelter were exactly like that, tails wagging the moment anyone walked past their kennel. Biscuit was different. He was a medium sized brown dog who pressed himself against the back wall of his kennel whenever a volunteer approached, refusing to make eye contact with anyone. The shelter staff told me he had been surrendered by a family that could no longer keep him, and that he had been there for almost four months without anyone showing serious interest in adopting him.

On my second day, I was assigned to sit quietly near Biscuit's kennel for fifteen minutes without trying to touch him or coax him toward me. It felt strange and a little pointless at first, just sitting on the cold concrete floor reading a book out loud to a dog that would not even look at me. The shelter coordinator, Mr. Ferreira, explained that some dogs need to learn that a person's presence does not automatically mean something bad is about to happen, and that trust sometimes has to be earned slowly, without any pressure at all.

I kept doing this every day for the rest of the week, reading whatever book I happened to be carrying, never expecting Biscuit to react differently. On my sixth visit, without any warning, he crept forward and rested his head near the edge of the kennel closest to where I sat, still not quite touching me, but noticeably closer than before. I did not move or make a sound, afraid that any sudden reaction might undo whatever small progress had just happened.

By the end of the two weeks, Biscuit would walk over and sit beside the kennel door whenever he heard my voice in the hallway, though he still flinched at loud noises and unfamiliar visitors. Mr. Ferreira told me that the quiet, patient time I had spent with him mattered more than any of the more obviously exciting tasks around the shelter, like walking the friendlier dogs or cleaning kennels.

I found out in February, almost a month after winter break ended, that a family had adopted Biscuit. Mr. Ferreira mentioned that they specifically noted he seemed comfortable with quiet visitors who did not rush him. I like to think that the fifteen minutes I spent sitting on cold concrete, reading books out loud to a dog who would not look at me, had something to do with that.

  1. The correct answer is C (he was fearful around people and had not attracted serious adoption interest): the passage describes him pressing against the kennel wall and avoiding eye contact, directly connecting his fearful behaviour to the lack of adoption interest. Option A is never mentioned anywhere in the passage. Options B and D invent reasons that the passage never actually gives.
  2. The correct answer is D (he believed Biscuit needed to build trust gradually without feeling any pressure): Mr. Ferreira explains this idea directly, saying some dogs need to learn that a person's presence is not automatically threatening. Option A is never suggested anywhere in the passage. Options B and C introduce reasons about testing volunteers or protecting other dogs that the passage never actually mentions.
  3. The correct answer is B (slowly earning the trust of a shy new classmate over several weeks): this closely mirrors the slow, patient process Sofia used to build trust with a fearful animal over time. Option A describes a quick, unrelated activity with no patience involved. Options C and D describe fast paced situations that do not involve any gradual trust building at all.
  4. The correct answer is A (reverse or cancel out the trust that had slowly begun to develop): this fits the context directly, since Biscuit had just crept slightly closer for the first time, and Sofia feared losing that fragile progress. Option B misreads the sentence as describing physical harm rather than a setback in trust. Options C and D introduce consequences that the passage never actually mentions.
  5. The correct answer is C (on her sixth visit, after nearly a week of quiet, patient sessions): the passage states this directly, describing the sixth visit as the moment Biscuit first crept forward. Option A contradicts the passage, since Biscuit does not react this way until much later. Options B and D describe timing that does not match the sequence of events the passage lays out.
  6. The correct answer is D (quiet, patient effort can matter just as much as more obviously active tasks): Mr. Ferreira states this directly, telling Sofia that her patient sessions mattered more than more obviously exciting shelter tasks. Option A is never suggested anywhere in the passage. Options B and C describe ideas the passage never actually raises or supports.
  7. The correct answer is A (to suggest that her patient approach may have genuinely helped Biscuit in the end): the closing lines connect her quiet visits directly to Biscuit later being comfortable with quiet, unhurried people. Option B is never mentioned anywhere in the passage. Options C and D overstate what the passage actually claims about reading aloud and about Biscuit fully overcoming his fears.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

Passage real_life_narrative_43

I joined the photography club at Eastview High mostly because my schedule had an empty spot during lunch on Wednesdays and my friend Priya said the club always had good snacks. I had a decent phone camera and had taken plenty of pictures of sunsets and my cat, but I had never thought of photography as something I could actually be good at, let alone something worth entering into a contest.

Mr. Okafor, who ran the club, announced in October that the school board was holding its annual Youth Perspectives photography contest, open to any student willing to submit a single photo along with a short paragraph explaining its meaning. The theme that year was simply belonging. Several members of the club immediately started planning elaborate shots involving group friendships or big community events, the kind of images that obviously matched the theme.

I could not think of anything that felt right. Every idea I had seemed like something someone else would already be submitting. For almost three weeks, my camera roll stayed mostly empty except for the usual sunsets. Priya suggested I stop trying to think of the perfect concept and instead just start taking pictures of ordinary moments from my actual life, trusting that meaning would show up on its own rather than being forced.

One evening in early November, I photographed my grandmother's hands while she taught me to knead dough for bread, the same recipe she had learned from her own mother decades earlier in a kitchen thousands of kilometres from ours. I had not been thinking about the contest at all when I took the picture. It was only afterward, looking at the photo on my phone, that I realized it captured something about belonging that felt more honest than any staged group photo could have.

I almost did not submit it, worried that a close up photo of flour covered hands would seem too small or too personal compared to the ambitious ideas other students had described. Priya convinced me to submit it anyway, along with a short paragraph about how the recipe connected three generations of women in my family across different countries and decades.

When the contest results were announced in December, my photo was selected as one of ten finalists displayed in the front hallway of the school for an entire month. Strangers I did not know stopped to read the paragraph beside my photo, and a few even told me it reminded them of their own grandparents. I did not win first place, but standing in that hallway watching people connect with something so personal to my own family taught me that the smallest, quietest moments sometimes carry the most meaning, if you are willing to actually look for them.

  1. The correct answer is B (the simple, broad theme of belonging, open to any interpretation): the passage states directly that the theme that year was simply belonging. Option A describes only one example idea other students chose, not the actual theme itself. Options C and D describe ideas related to Leah's own photo, not the official contest theme.
  2. The correct answer is A (she felt pressure to find a perfect concept and kept rejecting her own ideas): the passage states directly that every idea she had seemed like something someone else would already submit. Option B is contradicted, since she clearly wanted to participate but struggled with finding an idea. Options C and D introduce explanations that the passage never actually mentions.
  3. The correct answer is D (sharing something personal and creative, worried it might seem too small): this directly mirrors Leah's own hesitation about her close up, personal photo compared to more ambitious ideas from her classmates. Option A involves no personal vulnerability or creative risk at all. Options B and C describe unrelated activities with no comparable hesitation involved.
  4. The correct answer is C (artificially created or planned rather than arising naturally on its own): this fits Priya's advice directly, suggesting Leah stop overthinking a concept and instead photograph ordinary, genuine moments. Option A introduces a physical difficulty that is never actually discussed. Options B and D introduce ideas about rules and cost that the passage never mentions.
  5. The correct answer is B (one evening in early November, without thinking about the contest at all): the passage states this timing directly, along with the fact that she was not thinking about the contest when she took the photo. Option A contradicts the passage, since she struggled to find an idea for weeks after October. Options C and D describe events or timing that the passage never actually mentions.
  6. The correct answer is D (quiet, personal moments can carry meaning that connects with other people too): the closing paragraph states this directly, describing strangers connecting with her personal photo. Option A is contradicted, since her small, personal photo was selected over more elaborate ideas. Options B and C are directly contradicted, since strangers did connect with the photo and Leah values the experience despite not winning first place.
  7. Passage real_life_narrative_50

My first shift as a cashier at Fresh Market started with a fifteen minute training video and ended with me nearly in tears in the staff room, though I did not let anyone see that part. I was sixteen, it was my very first job, and I had assumed scanning groceries and making small talk with customers would be simple enough that I would master it within a day or two.

Then, on a busy Saturday afternoon during my second week, an older man came through my line with a cart full of groceries and became increasingly frustrated when I could not get a coupon to scan correctly. He raised his voice, told me I clearly had no idea what I was doing, and said younger cashiers should not be allowed to work the register alone.

I felt my face get hot and my hands start shaking slightly as I tried again with the coupon, which still refused to scan. Mrs. Petrov noticed from two registers away and came over immediately, calmly explaining to the man that the coupon had actually expired the previous week, which was the real reason it would not scan, not any mistake on my part. The man left without apologizing, still muttering under his breath as he walked toward the exit doors.

Once he was gone, Mrs. Petrov did not immediately return to her own register. She told me that some customers bring frustration from somewhere else entirely and unfairly direct it at whoever happens to be in front of them, and that this had nothing to do with whether I was doing my job correctly. She reminded me that staying calm and continuing to be polite, even when a customer is not, is one of the hardest and most important parts of working with the public.

Over the following weeks, similar situations happened again, though never quite as intense as that first one. Each time, I found it a little easier to stay calm, reminding myself of what Mrs. Petrov had said instead of assuming every complaint meant I had genuinely failed at something. I noticed I stopped taking rude comments personally in the same way, and started focusing instead on whether I had actually done my part of the job correctly.

By the end of the summer, Mrs. Petrov told the store manager she wanted me trained to help newer cashiers during their first difficult customer interactions, since she felt I had learned to handle those moments better than most sixteen year olds she had trained before. I still remember that first Saturday afternoon clearly, mostly because it taught me that patience is a skill you build gradually, one uncomfortable moment at a time.

I had plans to video call my friends from my cabin that weekend and had specifically told them I would still be reachable, not realizing my dad had chosen a site precisely because it had no service at all.

By the second morning, bored enough that even organizing our cooler seemed appealing, I agreed to join Toby and my dad on a hike around the lake that my dad had been describing enthusiastically since we left home. Without my phone constantly buzzing with notifications, I noticed things I probably would have scrolled past entirely at home, like the exact moment a loon called out across the water or the way morning light moved slowly across the surface of the lake as the sun rose higher.

Toby, it turned out, was full of surprisingly detailed questions about everything we passed, from why certain trees had bark that peeled in strips to whether fish could hear us talking from the shore. I found myself actually answering him instead of giving the short, distracted responses I usually gave at home while half paying attention to my phone. By the time we reached the halfway point of the trail, we had spent almost two straight hours talking without a single interruption from either of our devices.

That evening, sitting around the campfire that Toby had proudly helped build almost entirely by himself, my dad told us he had chosen this particular campground on purpose, specifically because it had no cell service. He said he had noticed over the past year that our family dinners had become quieter, with everyone half distracted by their phones, and he wanted at least one weekend where that simply was not possible for any of us.

I did not fully admit it to him that night, but he was right to notice. By the time we drove home on Monday, sunburned and covered in bug bites, I realized I had learned more about my little brother during that one weekend without service than I had during several months of ordinary weeknights at home, phones fully charged and constantly within reach.

Passage real_life_narrative_64

Every afternoon for three weeks, I pushed my skateboard up the ramp at Cedarbrook Skatepark and tried the same trick: a simple kickflip. Every afternoon, I landed on my hip instead of my wheels. My older brother, Malik, sat on the bench nearby, watching without saying much, which somehow made the failures feel louder.

The park was busiest around four o'clock, right after the middle school let out, so I usually had an audience of at least a dozen kids whether I wanted one or not. That Thursday, a group of younger skaters had gathered near the quarter pipe, and I could feel their eyes on me as I set up for another attempt. My hands were sweating even though the October air was cool.

I pushed off, flicked my foot the way I had watched a hundred videos explain, and the board spun beneath me exactly like it was supposed to. For half a second, I actually believed I had done it. Then my back foot missed the tail, and I crashed sideways onto the concrete, scraping my palm badly enough that it started to bleed almost right away.

A couple of the younger kids laughed, not unkindly, just the way people laugh when someone falls down in a cartoon. I wanted to grab my board and walk straight to the bus stop and never come back. Instead, I sat on the ground for a second longer than I needed to, mostly because my hip hurt and partly because I didn't trust my legs yet.

Malik walked over, not fast, and crouched down next to me. He didn't ask if I was okay, which I appreciated, because obviously I wasn't fully okay. Instead he said, 'You know how many times I ate concrete before I landed my first ollie? You weren't even born yet, and I was still falling.' I laughed despite myself, and it stung my scraped hand a little.

'Everyone here has watched somebody wipe out,' he added, nodding toward the group of younger kids, who had already moved on to their own tricks and weren't paying attention to me anymore. 'Nobody remembers the fall. They remember whether you got back up.'

I wrapped a tissue around my palm, grabbed my board, and rolled back toward the ramp. My legs were shaking, and I still felt the ghost of every kid's laughter, but underneath that there was something steadier taking hold. I set up for the kickflip one more time. I didn't land it that day either, not once, but on my sixth try I stayed on my feet for a full second longer than before, and that felt like enough.

By the time Malik and I walked home as the streetlights came on, my hand had stopped bleeding and my hip had stopped complaining quite so much. I hadn't landed the trick I came for, but I had figured out something more useful: the fear of falling in front of people had been bigger in my head than it ever was at the actual skatepark.

Option C is not stated in the passage and assumes something about the younger kids that isn't described. Option D narrows Malik's meaning too much to only avoiding laughter, missing the broader point about resilience.
  3. The correct answer is D (three weeks of daily practice at the skatepark): the very first sentence of the passage states that the narrator had been going to the skatepark every afternoon for three weeks trying the same trick. Option A is far too short and contradicts the stated timeline. Option B and option C both understate the length of time described, since the passage specifically names three full weeks rather than one or two.

Option D describes an empty, audience-free setting, which is the opposite of the crowded skatepark scene.

Passage real_life_narrative_71

Priya had rehearsed her first shift at Fresh Valley Grocery so many times in her head that when she finally clocked in on Saturday morning, the real thing felt strangely unfamiliar. Her manager, Mr. Okonkwo, gave her a five-minute tour of the store, showed her how to log into the cash register, and then walked away to deal with a delivery truck, leaving her alone at register three with a line already forming.

The first customer was an older man buying exactly one item, a carton of eggs, which should have been simple. Priya scanned it twice by accident, then had to call over another cashier to void the extra charge, and the whole process took almost four minutes while the man tapped his fingers on the counter. By the time he left, her hands were shaking slightly, and she still had six people waiting.

Things did not improve much through the morning. She bagged a jar of pasta sauce on top of a loaf of bread and crushed it flat. She forgot the code for produce twice and had to ask the woman behind the customer for help identifying a bag of parsnips. Around noon, during a brief lull, she found herself blinking back tears near the receipt paper roll, certain that everyone in the store had noticed how badly she was struggling.

That was when Denise, a cashier who had worked at Fresh Valley for six years, walked over from register one and started restocking bags beside her without being asked. 'Your first Saturday is always the worst one,' Denise said, not looking up from the bags. 'I once gave a customer forty dollars too much change because I mixed up a five and a fifty. He came back and returned it, which honestly surprised me more than the mistake did.'

Priya laughed for the first time all day, and something in her chest loosened slightly. Denise showed her a trick for remembering the produce codes, taping a small cheat sheet under the edge of the register where only the cashier could see it. She also reminded Priya that customers rarely remember an awkward cashier the way the cashier remembers themselves.

By her last hour, Priya still made mistakes, misreading a coupon and scanning someone's loyalty card twice, but she noticed she was recovering from each one faster, without the same flood of panic. When Mr. Okonkwo came by to close out her till at the end of the shift, he glanced at her total and said simply, 'Not bad for a first day,' before moving on to the next task.

Walking to the bus stop afterward, her feet aching in a way they never had before, Priya realized the version of the shift she had rehearsed in her head, the one where everything went smoothly from the first minute, had never actually been realistic. What she had gotten instead, a rough morning followed by a slightly steadier afternoon, felt more like something she could actually build on.

Passage real_life_narrative_78

Owen almost quit the robotics club three separate times before the regional competition, and each time it was for the same reason: the wiring for the claw arm refused to cooperate no matter what he tried. He had joined in September mostly because his friend Tasha begged him to fill an empty spot on the four-person team, and by November he was starting to think it had been a mistake.

The team's robot, nicknamed Clunker by everyone except their coach, was supposed to pick up small foam blocks and stack them inside a target zone. In practice, the claw either gripped too loosely and dropped the blocks halfway across the room, or gripped so tightly that the motor strained and made a grinding sound that worried everyone within earshot. Owen had rewired the claw's circuit four times, and each version had a different problem.

Two weeks before the competition, during a Tuesday practice that was supposed to be their final major test, the claw dropped every single block it attempted to pick up, seven times in a row. Tasha threw her hands up and said maybe they should just enter with a robot that could only push blocks instead of lifting them, since at least pushing worked reliably. Their coach, Ms. Ferreira, didn't say anything for a long moment, just looked at the tangle of wires with her arms crossed.

Owen felt his face get hot, the particular kind of hot that comes right before frustrated tears, though he was determined not to let anyone see that. He grabbed his notebook and started sketching the circuit from scratch, ignoring the diagram he had been copying from a tutorial video that clearly wasn't built for their specific claw model. Tasha sat down beside him without a word and started double checking his math on the resistor values.

It took another hour, well past when the rest of the team had normally packed up to leave, but they eventually traced the problem to a single component: a sensor that was reading the claw's grip pressure incorrectly because it had been installed backward from the start. Once they flipped it, the claw picked up a block cleanly on the very first try, then a second, then a third.

Ms. Ferreira, who had stayed late along with them, allowed herself a small smile and said this was exactly the kind of debugging real engineers did constantly, quietly, without cameras or applause. At the competition itself the following week, Clunker's claw dropped only one block out of the fourteen it attempted, which was enough to place the team third out of eighteen schools.

Standing in the gymnasium afterward holding a small trophy that felt heavier than its size suggested, Owen realized the moments he would actually remember were not the parts where everything worked. They were the Tuesday night when nothing worked, and he had fixed it anyway.

The dog was shivering under the footbridge in Millbrook Ravine when I found it, a scruffy brown mutt with no collar and a limp in its back left leg. It was late October, already getting dark by six, and I had only gone down to the ravine because my mom had sent me to look for our cat, who turned out to be sitting on our own back porch the entire time.

I crouched down slowly, the way my aunt had once taught me with nervous animals, and held out my hand without moving closer. The dog flinched but didn't run, which I took as a small hopeful sign. Up close, it looked thin, its ribs slightly visible beneath its matted fur, and I guessed it had probably been out here for at least a few days.

I didn't have a leash, so I ended up using the drawstring from my hoodie, looping it gently around the dog's neck while talking to it in the calmest voice I could manage, even though my own heart was pounding. The walk home took twice as long as usual because the dog kept stopping to sniff every third fence post, and I kept glancing over my shoulder half expecting someone to come running after us claiming it was theirs.

My mom was not thrilled when I showed up with a strange, limping dog instead of our cat, but she softened once she saw how gently it leaned against my leg on the porch. We gave it water and a can of tuna, which it ate faster than seemed physically possible, and my mom called the number for animal control, only to be told the shelter was already full and it could be days before anyone could come check for a microchip.

For the next four days, the dog, who I had started calling Biscuit without really deciding to, slept on an old blanket in our laundry room. I posted photos on three different community boards, and on the third night, I got a message from a woman named Renata who lived across the ravine and had lost her dog, Toffee, nine days earlier after a thunderstorm spooked him out of her backyard.

When Renata arrived the next afternoon, Toffee, or Biscuit, or whatever his real name was in that moment, went completely still for a second and then practically threw himself against her legs, his tail moving so fast it was almost a blur. Renata cried, thanked me about six separate times, and offered to pay me, which I turned down, though I did accept the box of homemade cookies she brought back the following week.

After she left with Toffee trotting happily beside her, our laundry room felt strangely empty for a few days. I hadn't planned on getting attached to a dog I only had for four days, but I found myself checking the community board out of habit anyway, just in case somebody else's pet needed finding.

Passage real_life_narrative_92

Jade and Sam had been best friends since elementary school, which was exactly why their science fair project on water filtration had turned into such an uncomfortable mess by the second week of working on it. Sam wanted to build an elaborate multi-layer filter using sand, charcoal, and gravel, timed carefully to show gradual improvement in water clarity. Jade thought the whole plan was overly complicated for a project due in twelve days and wanted something simpler they could actually finish.

The disagreement started small, a few sharp comments while sketching diagrams at Jade's kitchen table, but it grew worse the following Tuesday when Sam showed up with an entire supply list that Jade had never agreed to and, more importantly, had never budgeted for. Jade said the plan was unrealistic. Sam said Jade never wanted to try anything ambitious. Neither of them said anything else for the rest of that afternoon, and Sam left twenty minutes early without the usual goodbye.

For three days, they barely spoke outside of the group chat, which had gone completely silent aside from a single message from Jade asking whether Sam still wanted to work together at all. Jade spent those three days assuming Sam was still furious, and Sam spent them assuming Jade thought the friendship wasn't worth the argument. Neither assumption turned out to be entirely true, but neither of them found that out until Friday.

At lunch, Jade finally sat down across from Sam instead of at their usual spot with other friends, and the two of them just looked at each other for a few seconds before Jade said, 'I don't actually think your idea is bad. I think I panicked about the deadline and took it out on you.' Sam admitted the supply list probably had been too ambitious, and that showing up with it unannounced had been a mistake rather than a thoughtful surprise.

They ended up compromising on a two-layer filter instead of Sam's original four layers, simple enough to finish with four days to spare but still complex enough that Sam felt proud of the design. Jade handled the written report and the measurements, while Sam built the physical filter and ran the trials, and for the first time in nearly a week, working together felt normal again rather than tense.

Their project placed second in the school fair, just behind a group that had used a much more expensive filtration kit bought online rather than built from scratch. Standing near their table afterward, Jade admitted she had spent more energy worrying about the argument itself than about actually losing the disagreement. Sam pointed out that the two-layer filter, the compromise neither of them had originally wanted, had ended up being the version they were both proudest of by the end.

Walking home together that afternoon, both of them agreed that the argument, as uncomfortable as it had been, had probably made the finished project better than either of their original plans alone would have.

Passage real_life_narrative_99

My grandmother lives two towns over, close enough that my parents usually just drive me there, but that Saturday they both had to work a double shift at the hospital, so it was decided, mostly by my mother, that I was old enough to take the regional bus there by myself for the first time. She wrote the route number on a sticky note, reminded me three separate times to text her when I got on and off, and then dropped me at the station twenty minutes before the bus was even scheduled to arrive.

I had never really paid attention to how loud a bus station could be, or how many different routes left from platforms that all looked nearly identical. I checked the sticky note against the overhead sign four times, convinced I was somehow reading it wrong, before I finally worked up the nerve to ask a woman standing nearby whether this was really the platform for route 12. She confirmed it was, and I felt my shoulders drop slightly from around my ears.

Once on the bus, I picked a window seat near the front, mostly because it felt safer somehow, though I couldn't have explained exactly why. About fifteen minutes into the ride, the bus took a turn I didn't remember from car trips with my parents, and a small wave of panic rose in my chest. I pulled up a map on my phone, and my hands were shaking enough that it took two tries to zoom in properly.

The map showed the bus was still on route, just taking a slightly different road because of construction, but knowing that logically didn't fully stop the nervous feeling in my stomach. I texted my mom anyway, just to hear back from someone, and she replied almost instantly with a single word: relax. It wasn't especially comforting in the moment, though looking back it probably should have been.

By the time the bus pulled into my grandmother's town, I had mostly calmed down, helped along by an older man across the aisle who noticed me checking my phone constantly and mentioned, without me even asking, that this route always took the detour on weekends. My grandmother was waiting exactly where she said she would be, right outside the small brick station, and she laughed when she saw how tightly I was gripping my backpack straps.

Over lunch, she asked how the ride had gone, and I found myself describing the whole thing, the construction detour and the panic and the stranger who had reassured me, as though it had been a much bigger adventure than it probably actually was. She told me that the first time she rode a bus alone, she had gotten off two stops early by mistake and had to walk nearly a kilometre in the rain.

Riding the bus home that evening felt almost boring in comparison, which surprised me more than anything else that day.

Passage real_life_narrative_106

Devon had signed up for the talent show back in September, when performing a violin solo in front of the entire school had felt like a distant, almost theoretical problem. By the second week of April, with the actual performance only three days away, it no longer felt distant at all. Devon had practiced the piece, a shortened arrangement of a piece by Vivaldi, dozens of times in the basement, and could play it nearly perfectly there. The gymnasium stage was an entirely different story.

During the dress rehearsal on Tuesday, Devon walked out under the stage lights, lifted the violin, and immediately forgot the opening notes completely, standing frozen for what felt like several minutes but was probably closer to ten seconds. Ms. Alvarez, the music teacher running the rehearsal, called out the first note gently from the front row, which was enough to get Devon started again, though the rest of the run-through was shaky at best.

Walking home afterward, Devon seriously considered telling Ms. Alvarez the next morning that something had come up and the performance would need to be cancelled. That plan lasted until dinner, when Devon's older cousin Priya, visiting for the week, asked how rehearsal had gone. Devon admitted the whole humiliating story, expecting sympathy or maybe advice about breathing exercises.

Instead, Priya laughed, not meanly, and said she had once forgotten an entire dance routine during a recital in front of nearly two hundred people, and had simply improvised different steps until the music ended, badly enough that her teacher mentioned it for years afterward as a cautionary tale. 'Nobody in that gym is going to remember your talent show performance in five years,' Priya said. 'You'll remember it either way, so you might as well make it a story where you actually go through with it.'

That night, Devon practiced differently, not focusing on playing the piece perfectly but specifically practicing what to do if the memory blanked again: which note to guess at, how to keep the bow moving even while thinking, how to avoid stopping completely no matter what. It felt strange to practice for failure instead of success, but it also felt oddly calming, like defusing something that had seemed unpredictable before.

On the night of the actual talent show, Devon's hands were shaking noticeably while waiting backstage, and for one terrible second walking out onto the stage, the opening notes threatened to disappear again exactly like during rehearsal. This time, though, Devon kept the bow moving anyway, playing something close enough to the correct notes that most of the audience likely never noticed the brief wobble in the middle of the piece.

The applause afterward wasn't the loudest of the night, but it was steady, and from the audience Devon could see Priya standing and clapping harder than seemed strictly necessary. Backstage, breathing hard and feeling strangely proud despite the mistakes, Devon realized the actual performance had mattered far less than simply deciding to walk out onto that stage at all.

  1. Option A is directly contradicted by this freeze. Option C and option D are not described as happening during the rehearsal itself.
  2. Option A, option B, and option C are not what Priya is trying to communicate through her story.
  3. The correct answer is C (choosing to go through with something despite fear matters more than a flawless result): the final paragraph states the actual performance mattered far less than simply deciding to walk out onto the stage. Option A is contradicted, since perfect basement practice did not prevent freezing on stage. Option B is an unrealistic standard never suggested by the passage. Option D shifts responsibility away from Devon, which the passage does not support.
  4. The correct answer is A (what to do if the memory of the piece goes blank again): the passage states Devon specifically practiced what to do if the memory blanked again, including which notes to guess and how to keep the bow moving. Option B, option C, and option D are not mentioned as part of this new practice approach.
  5. The correct answer is D (feeling nervous before doing something in front of others after a bad rehearsal): this matches Devon's exact situation, standing backstage nervous after a difficult rehearsal experience. Option A and option B describe calm, low-pressure situations that do not match Devon's nervousness. Option C removes Devon from the performing role entirely.
  6. Passage real_life_narrative_113

When my dad's back injury put him on strict bed rest for three weeks, somebody had to start cooking dinner, and since my older sister was away at a volleyball tournament that first week, that somebody turned out to be me. Before that, my cooking experience mostly involved reheating leftovers and making toast, so the idea of preparing an actual meal for four people felt genuinely overwhelming.

The first night, I attempted spaghetti because it seemed like the simplest possible option, and I still managed to overcook the noodles into something closer to paste while somehow undercooking the sauce, leaving it watery and bland. My mom, exhausted from working a full day and then checking on my dad every hour, ate it anyway without complaining, though my younger brother made a face he clearly thought was subtle.

I called my grandmother that night, mostly out of desperation, and she talked me through some basic timing mistakes over speakerphone while I stood in the kitchen with a notepad. She reminded me that pasta keeps cooking a little even after you drain it, and that sauce needs time to actually reduce and thicken rather than being dumped on immediately. It sounded obvious once she explained it, though it clearly hadn't been obvious to me an hour earlier.

Over the following two weeks, I worked my way through a short list of dishes my grandmother walked me through one at a time: a simple stir fry, baked chicken with vegetables, a lentil soup that took nearly two hours but mostly just sat on the stove without needing much attention from me. Some nights went fine. One memorable Wednesday, I nearly set off the smoke alarm trying to fry rice at too high a heat, and my dad, forbidden from getting out of bed, shouted cooking advice down the hallway that I could barely understand.

By the middle of the third week, dinner had stopped feeling like a crisis I was barely surviving and started feeling like an actual task I could handle, even if it wasn't always exciting. My mom mentioned one evening, almost offhand, that the lentil soup was better than the version she usually made, which surprised me enough that I made her repeat it.

When my dad was finally cleared to stand in the kitchen again, though still not allowed to lift anything heavy, he asked me to make the stir fry so he could watch how I did it, correcting my knife grip but otherwise mostly just observing. Afterward he admitted he hadn't expected me to actually stick with cooking dinner every night rather than just ordering food or reheating something from the freezer.

Looking back at those three weeks now, I don't think I became some kind of skilled cook overnight, but I stopped being someone who was afraid of the kitchen, which felt like its own kind of accomplishment by the time my dad was finally back on his feet.

Option A is an unrealistic claim never made in the passage. Option C and option D are not supported anywhere by the narrator's reflections.
  4. The correct answer is C (taking on a new responsibility you feel unprepared for and slowly improving): this closely matches the narrator's experience learning to cook dinner under pressure. Option A and option B involve no active responsibility at all. Option D describes something already familiar, unlike the narrator's initial struggle.
  5. Option B, option C, and option D are not mentioned anywhere in the passage as advice given.
  6. The correct answer is D (the narrator's cooking skills have genuinely improved during this time): the narrator's surprised reaction and the sincerity of the comment support this reading.   7. The correct answer is B (he watches the narrator cook and corrects their knife grip): the passage states he asked the narrator to make the stir fry so he could watch, correcting the knife grip while mostly observing. Option A, option C, and option D are not described anywhere in the passage.
Passage real_life_narrative_120

Aisha had been training for the community five kilometre fun run for almost two months when a rolled ankle during gym class threatened to end her plans two weeks before race day. She had started training in July mostly on a whim, after her cousin mentioned the race almost casually at a family barbecue, but somewhere around week three the whim had turned into something she actually cared about finishing.

The ankle injury happened during a routine game of volleyball, an awkward landing that made a small popping sound she felt more than heard. The school nurse wrapped it in ice and told her it was likely just a mild sprain rather than anything more serious, but the doctor her mother took her to that evening was firm that Aisha needed to stay off it completely for at least a week, maybe longer depending on how it healed.

A week of no running felt unbearable at first, especially watching her training plan sit untouched on the fridge, each missed day marked with an increasingly frustrated mental tally. She spent most evenings icing her ankle on the couch while her older brother, who had run several races himself, reminded her more than once that rushing back too early was the surest way to make an injury worse rather than better.

By the second week, the swelling had gone down enough that her doctor cleared her for light walking, though running was still off limits until closer to the race itself, which was now only five days away. Aisha spent those days doing gentle stretches and short walks around the block, trying not to calculate exactly how much fitness she had probably lost during two weeks of complete rest.

Two days before the race, she attempted a slow, careful jog around the block, moving far more cautiously than her usual pace, testing whether the ankle could handle any real pressure at all. It held, though it ached slightly by the end, enough that she decided not to push any further before race day itself. Her brother suggested she plan to walk the race if needed rather than risk running the whole thing and reinjuring the ankle partway through.

On the morning of the race, standing near the starting line surrounded by dozens of other runners stretching and chatting nervously, Aisha felt a familiar knot of anxiety, made worse by the ankle brace she had strapped on that morning just in case. She decided before the starting horn even sounded that she would run when she comfortably could and walk the moment anything felt wrong, rather than trying to prove something to herself by pushing through pain.

She ended up finishing in just over thirty four minutes, slower than the pace she had trained for before the injury, having walked two short stretches when her ankle started to twinge. Crossing the finish line, tired and a little disappointed by her time, she still felt an unexpected wave of pride, mostly because two weeks earlier she genuinely hadn't been sure she would be able to finish the race at all.

  3. Option A is contradicted by her genuine pride despite a slower time. Option B is contradicted by Aisha successfully finishing the race. Option C is never suggested anywhere in the passage.
  4. Option A describes the opposite of what happens to Aisha. Option C contradicts Aisha's decision to still attempt the race. Option D removes Aisha from the experience entirely.
  5. The correct answer is C (to run when comfortable and walk if anything feels wrong): the passage states she decided before the horn sounded that she would run when comfortable and walk if anything felt wrong. Option A, option B, and option D are not supported anywhere in the passage.
  6.   7. The correct answer is D (an ankle brace strapped on before the race began): the passage states her anxiety was made worse by the ankle brace she had strapped on that morning just in case. Option A, option B, and option C are not mentioned anywhere in the passage.
Passage real_life_narrative_127

I had circled the date on my calendar three weeks before basketball tryouts even started. Every afternoon after school, I dribbled a ball around our driveway until the streetlights came on, practicing crossovers I had watched a hundred times online. My older brother, Malcolm, used to play for our school, and I wanted nothing more than to wear the same navy jersey he once wore.

On the first day of tryouts, the gym felt smaller than I remembered, packed with almost forty kids all fighting for twelve spots. Coach Reyes blew her whistle and split us into groups for layup drills. My hands were sweating so badly that I fumbled the ball twice before I even took a shot. I could feel my face turning red as a few of the older kids snickered behind me.

By the second drill, a full-court scrimmage, I had settled down enough to make a few decent passes, but I missed an easy shot under the basket that would have tied the score for my team. Coach Reyes didn't yell. She just wrote something on her clipboard and moved on to watch the next group. I spent the whole bus ride home replaying that missed shot over and over, certain I had blown my only chance.

When the list of names went up on the gym door two days later, I scanned it three times before I finally accepted that mine wasn't there. I didn't make the team. I stood in the hallway holding my backpack, trying to figure out how I would explain this to Malcolm, when Coach Reyes came out of her office and asked me to wait a moment.

She told me something I hadn't expected. She said she remembered exactly which drill I had struggled with, not because I was bad, but because of what I did right after missing that shot. Instead of sulking, I had sprinted back on defence and stolen the ball from the other team. She said that kind of hustle is harder to teach than shooting, and she invited me to join the junior practice squad that trains with the main team twice a week, even though I wouldn't be dressing for games yet.

I almost said no. Practicing without ever playing in a real game sounded like a consolation prize, and part of me wanted to just quit and try out for something else next year. But then I thought about how much I actually loved being in that gym, the squeak of sneakers on the floor and the sound of the ball hitting the rim. I told Coach Reyes I would be there Monday.

Three months later, one of the starting guards sprained his ankle right before the biggest game of the season. Coach Reyes called my name off the bench in the third quarter. My legs were shaking as I ran onto the court, but I remembered exactly what she had told me in that hallway. I didn't need to be the best shooter in the gym. I just needed to give everything I had to every single play. We won that game by four points, and afterward Malcolm told me it was the proudest he had ever been of me. I still think about that missed shot sometimes, and how close I came to walking away before I ever got the chance to prove what I could do.

Option D contradicts the passage, since both settings clearly help the narrator improve.

Passage real_life_narrative_134

The bell above the door of Sunrise Bakery jingled every time a customer walked in, and by my third shift I still hadn't gotten used to how loud it sounded compared to the quiet of my own kitchen at home. I had begged my parents for weeks to let me get a part-time job, mostly because I wanted to save up for a new bike, and Sunrise Bakery was the only place within walking distance that was hiring someone my age.

My manager, Mrs. Okafor, had trained me on the register during my first two shifts, but she still stood beside me most mornings just in case. On the third Saturday, she finally stepped away to help in the kitchen, telling me I was ready to handle the counter alone during the slower afternoon hours. I felt a mix of pride and nervousness as I watched her disappear behind the swinging door.

Everything went smoothly until a man in a business suit rushed in, glancing at his phone, and ordered two dozen assorted pastries for a meeting that apparently started in ten minutes. I grabbed boxes and started filling them as fast as I could, but I mixed up his order, giving him plain croissants instead of the almond ones he had specifically asked for. He didn't notice until he was already back at his office, and he called the bakery furious, demanding a refund and complaining loudly enough that Mrs. Okafor could hear his voice through the phone from across the counter.

I felt sick to my stomach as she hung up. I was certain I was about to be fired on the spot, and I started rehearsing an apology in my head before she even said anything. Instead, Mrs. Okafor asked me to walk her through exactly what had happened, step by step, without getting upset or making excuses. When I explained how rushed and flustered I had felt, she nodded and said that every new employee makes a mistake like this eventually, and that what mattered now was what I did next.

She had me personally box a fresh dozen almond croissants and deliver them to the man's office two blocks away, along with a handwritten note apologizing for the mix-up. My hands were shaking as I handed the box to his assistant, but I felt a strange sense of relief once it was done, like I had actually fixed something instead of just being scolded for breaking it.

The following week, that same man came back into the bakery, and I froze behind the register, bracing myself for another complaint. Instead, he said the croissants I had delivered were the best he had ever tasted and asked if I could recommend something for his daughter's birthday. I realized then that Mrs. Okafor hadn't just been protecting the bakery's reputation when she sent me to fix my mistake. She had been teaching me that owning up to an error and doing the work to correct it could turn even an angry customer into someone who trusted you again.

Option D also does not connect to the specific lesson about correcting an error.
  4. Every morning on my walk to the bus stop, I passed by Fenwick Park, and every morning I noticed a little more garbage collecting along the edges of the walking path. Plastic bottles, fast food wrappers, and once even an old couch cushion had been left near the swings where my little cousin liked to play. I expected maybe two or three replies, mostly from people telling me it was a nice idea but that they were busy. Instead, within an hour, over twenty people had responded, including several parents, a couple of teachers from my school, and even Mr. Halloran, who ran the corner store and offered to donate garbage bags and work gloves for everyone.
