We spent nearly three hours picking up trash, and by the end, we had filled twenty-two large garbage bags and even hauled that old couch cushion to the curb for pickup. What surprised me most wasn't how much garbage we collected, but how many neighbours I met for the first time that day, people who lived just a few houses away from me but whom I had never once spoken to before.

Dario joked that we should put our own names on the sign instead, but honestly, I didn't care about getting credit. What stuck with me was realizing that a problem I had walked past and complained about for months could actually be fixed, and that most people are willing to help make their neighbourhood better if someone is simply willing to ask them and organize the effort.

Ever since fourth grade, Talia and I had done almost everything together, so when our English teacher paired us up for a semester-long project on graphic novels, I assumed it would be the easiest assignment of the year. The trouble started when our deadline moved up unexpectedly because of a school assembly schedule change, giving us only four days instead of two full weeks to finish the final version.

She had stayed up nearly as late as I had, working around her recital rehearsal, without telling me because she was still upset about how dismissive I had been of her schedule. Seeing the finished pages made me feel worse, not better, because I realized how unfair I had been to assume she wasn't taking the project seriously just because she couldn't meet my unreasonable timeline.

She admitted she probably should have told me about the added pressure from the schedule change instead of going quiet and finishing everything alone. We agreed that next time either of us felt overwhelmed by a deadline, we would say so directly instead of letting frustration build silently between us.

Looking back, I think that project taught me more about friendship than about graphic novels. Talia and I are still close now, but we communicate differently than we used to, checking in with each other honestly whenever something feels unfair, instead of assuming the other person will just figure it out on their own.

Passage real_life_narrative_155

My cousin Ezra had been skateboarding for almost three years by the time he convinced me to try it, mostly by promising he wouldn't laugh no matter how many times I fell. I had watched him land tricks at the skate park near our building for what felt like forever, always from a safe distance on the bench, telling myself I would try it eventually once I felt more coordinated.

The truth was, I had been putting it off because I was genuinely afraid of getting hurt. My older sister had broken her wrist rollerblading two summers earlier, and the memory of her cast stuck with me longer than I ever admitted out loud. When Ezra finally handed me his spare board one Saturday morning, my hands were shaking before I even stepped onto it.

The first few attempts were exactly as clumsy as I expected. I couldn't even roll in a straight line without wobbling off balance and stepping down early, and after about twenty minutes I was ready to give the board back and call it a failed experiment. Ezra, though, refused to let me quit that easily. He suggested I practice just standing still on the board first, getting comfortable with the feeling before worrying about actually rolling anywhere.

That small adjustment made a bigger difference than I expected. By the end of the first hour, I could roll slowly across the flat part of the parking lot without immediately losing my balance. It still felt nowhere close to what Ezra could do, but for the first time, it felt like something I might actually be capable of learning instead of something reserved only for naturally athletic people like him.

Over the following month, I showed up to the parking lot almost every weekend, gradually working up the confidence to try the small ramp near the edge of the skate park. My first three attempts ended with me jumping off awkwardly before reaching the top, too nervous to commit fully to the motion. On my fourth try, I finally pushed through that hesitation, rolled up the ramp, and came back down without falling, letting out a shout of surprise that made a few younger kids nearby laugh and clap.

I still fell plenty of times after that, including one memorable wipeout that left a bruise on my elbow for almost two weeks. What surprised me most, though, was how much less scary each fall became once I understood that falling was simply part of learning the sport, not a sign that I was failing at it entirely. Ezra told me that even he still falls sometimes when trying a trick he hasn't fully mastered yet, which made me feel less embarrassed about my own wobbly progress.

Now, almost a year later, I can ride comfortably around the whole skate park and even land a basic ollie most of the time. I don't think I'll ever be quite as fearless as Ezra on a board, but I've stopped comparing my progress to his entirely. What matters more to me now is how far I've come from that first shaky Saturday morning, when I could barely stand on the board without gripping Ezra's arm for balance.

Conversations at dinner started lasting longer, not because anyone was trying harder, but simply because nobody kept getting interrupted by a buzz or a glance downward. My grandmother came back for dinner again the following Sunday, and this time I left my phone in the hallway drawer the entire evening without even thinking about it.

She told a much longer story that night about meeting my grandfather at a community dance decades ago, one I had never heard in that much detail before, and I realized I probably would have missed half of it if my phone had been sitting beside my plate. I still check my phone plenty throughout the day, but dinner has become the one time I've learned to actually put it away completely.

Public speaking had always made my hands go cold and my thoughts scatter the moment more than a few people looked directly at me, and this presentation counted for a large chunk of our final grade.

That small gesture was enough to jolt my memory back, and I managed to pick up where I had left off.

The rest of the presentation still wasn't perfect. My voice shook during a couple of sentences, and I spoke faster than I meant to near the end because I was eager to finish. But I made it through the whole thing without stopping again, and when I sat back down, Ms. Delacroix gave me a small, encouraging nod that made my shoulders relax for the first time since walking to the front of the room.

Later that day, Owen told me he used to freeze up during presentations too, back in elementary school, and that the only thing that had actually helped him improve was doing it over and over despite how uncomfortable it felt each time. I still don't think public speaking will ever come naturally to me, but I no longer feel like freezing up for a moment means the whole presentation is ruined. Sometimes it just means you need one small reminder to find your place again.

Passage information_paragraph_174

Skateboarding has grown from a niche hobby into one of the most popular activities among Canadian teens, and cities across Ontario have responded by building public skate parks in nearly every neighbourhood. A skate park is an outdoor or indoor space built with ramps, rails, ledges, and bowls that let skateboarders, in-line skaters, and BMX riders practise tricks safely away from busy streets and sidewalks. Before skate parks became common, many young people skated in parking lots, empty pools, or stairwells, which often led to conflicts with property owners and higher rates of injury.

The first modern skate parks in Ontario opened in the late 1990s, built mostly from donated concrete and volunteer labour. Today, most municipalities include skate parks in their official parks and recreation budgets, treating them the same way they treat soccer fields or baseball diamonds. A well-designed skate park usually separates beginner areas from advanced sections, so a new skater practising basic balance is not sharing space with someone attempting a difficult jump. Many parks also install rubber safety surfacing near the edges and require riders to wear helmets, following guidelines set by local recreation departments.

Skate parks offer more than just a place to practise tricks. Researchers who study youth recreation have found that skate parks give teens a low-cost, drop-in activity that does not require joining a team, paying registration fees, or committing to a fixed schedule. This makes skateboarding especially appealing to teens whose families cannot afford club sports or who prefer an activity they can start and stop on their own time. Skate parks also tend to build strong informal communities, since experienced skaters often mentor newcomers, showing them how to fall safely or land a trick they have been struggling with.

Funding for skate parks usually comes from a mix of municipal budgets, provincial recreation grants, and local fundraising campaigns organized by skateboarding clubs or concerned parents. In several Ontario towns, teenagers themselves have petitioned city council for a new or improved skate park, presenting petitions signed by hundreds of residents. Some of these campaigns have taken years to succeed, since municipal councils must balance skate park funding against requests for other recreational facilities like splash pads or hockey rinks.

Despite their popularity, skate parks are not without challenges. Noise complaints from nearby homeowners, vandalism, and the ongoing cost of repairing worn-out ramps all require attention from city staff. Even so, most municipal recreation departments report that skate parks remain heavily used from early spring through late fall, and many are now exploring covered or indoor options so that skaters can use them safely through the winter months as well.

The passage explains that researchers found skate parks appealing to teens whose families cannot afford club sports because there are no registration fees or fixed schedules. Options A, B, and C all contradict this by describing costs or requirements the passage never mentions.

This response directly connects a real personal memory to the passage's point about long fundraising campaigns for skate parks. Passage information_paragraph_180

Community gardens have become a common sight in cities and towns across Ontario, transforming empty lots, schoolyard corners, and unused park space into shared spaces where neighbours grow vegetables, herbs, and flowers together. A community garden is typically divided into individual plots that residents can rent for a small annual fee, along with shared areas like compost bins, tool sheds, and water taps that everyone maintains together. Unlike a private backyard garden, a community garden depends on cooperation, since gardeners must agree on shared rules about watering schedules, pest control, and keeping pathways clear.

The idea of community gardening is not new. During the First and Second World Wars, Canadians were encouraged to plant "victory gardens" to grow their own food and reduce pressure on the national food supply. Many of today's community gardens trace their roots back to that same idea: growing your own food builds both practical skills and a sense of shared purpose. In recent years, community gardens have expanded well beyond food production. Some now include pollinator gardens planted specifically to support bees and butterflies, while others set aside plots for local schools so that students can learn about plant biology firsthand.

Local governments and non-profit organizations often work together to establish new community gardens. A neighbourhood group might approach the city with a proposal to convert an unused lot, and if approved, the city may provide soil, fencing, or water access while the group organizes volunteers to build the beds and recruit gardeners. Many gardens keep a waiting list, since demand for plots often outpaces the available space, especially in dense urban neighbourhoods where residents may not have a yard of their own.

Community gardens provide benefits that go beyond fresh produce. Studies on urban green spaces have found that gardening lowers stress, encourages physical activity, and helps neighbours who might never otherwise meet get to know each other. Older gardeners often pass along practical knowledge, such as when to plant certain vegetables or how to deal with a specific pest, to newer members who are just starting out. For teens especially, working a garden plot can be a low-cost way to spend time outdoors, learn a practical skill, and contribute food to a family's table or a local food bank.

Not every community garden succeeds right away. Poor soil, vandalism, or a shortage of volunteers willing to manage shared tasks can cause a garden to struggle in its first few seasons. Successful gardens usually have a clear set of written rules and at least one dedicated coordinator who resolves disputes and keeps the shared areas organized. When that structure is in place, a community garden can operate successfully for decades, becoming a lasting fixture of the neighbourhood it serves.

Competitive video gaming, often called esports, has grown from a small hobby community into a major form of entertainment watched by millions of people worldwide. Esports involves organized video game competitions, often between professional teams, played in front of live audiences or streamed online to fans. Popular esports titles include team-based strategy games, first-person shooters, and sports simulations, each with its own professional leagues, sponsors, and dedicated fan base.

The roots of esports stretch back to small arcade tournaments held in the 1980s, where players competed for high scores on games like Space Invaders. For decades, competitive gaming remained a niche activity known mainly to dedicated fans. That changed dramatically with the rise of high-speed internet and video streaming platforms, which made it possible for anyone with an internet connection to watch top players compete in real time, no matter where in the world they lived. Today, major esports tournaments fill entire arenas and offer prize pools worth millions of dollars, rivaling some traditional professional sports events.

Ontario has become an active part of this growth. Several Ontario high schools and universities now offer varsity esports programs, complete with coaches, practice schedules, and scholarships for skilled players. Some school boards have added esports as an official extracurricular activity, treating it similarly to basketball or volleyball, with tryouts and structured team practices. Supporters argue that esports teaches teamwork, quick decision-making, and communication skills that transfer well to other areas of life, much like traditional team sports do.

Despite its growing acceptance, esports still faces questions that traditional sports rarely deal with. Concerns about excessive screen time, the physical strain of long practice sessions, and the pressure young players face to perform for an online audience are all topics that researchers and parents continue to debate. Some professional esports organizations have responded by hiring sports psychologists and physical trainers, treating player wellness as seriously as any traditional sports team would.

For many teens, esports offers an entry point into gaming as a possible career path, whether as a player, a broadcaster, a coach, or an event organizer. Post-secondary programs in game design, sports management, and digital broadcasting increasingly include esports-specific courses, reflecting how quickly the industry has grown. While not every young player will go on to compete professionally, the skills built through organized esports, including strategy, cooperation, and composure under pressure, can carry over into many other pursuits long after a person stops playing competitively.

Passage information_paragraph_192

Public libraries across Ontario have expanded far beyond their traditional role of lending books, adding spaces called makerspaces where visitors can use tools and technology to design and build their own projects. A makerspace is an area within a library equipped with equipment such as 3D printers, sewing machines, sound recording booths, or laser cutters, along with staff trained to help visitors learn to use them safely. Anyone with a library card can typically book time in a makerspace at no extra cost, which sets it apart from private workshops that often charge membership fees.

The makerspace movement began in the early 2010s as libraries looked for ways to stay relevant in a world where more information was available online than ever before. Rather than competing with the internet for information, many libraries decided to offer something the internet could not: free, hands-on access to expensive equipment that most families could not afford to own themselves. A student interested in fashion design might use a library sewing machine to create a costume, while another visitor might use a 3D printer to build a part for a science fair project, all without spending money on their own equipment.

Makerspaces have proven especially valuable for teens working on school projects or personal hobbies. Library staff who run these spaces often hold workshops teaching basic skills, such as how to use design software before printing a 3D object, or how to edit an audio recording after using a sound booth. Some libraries partner with local high schools, allowing classes to visit the makerspace during the school day to work on assignments that require specialized equipment the school itself does not have.

Running a makerspace comes with real challenges for library staff. Equipment like 3D printers and laser cutters requires regular maintenance and can be expensive to repair when something breaks. Staff must also be trained not just in library science but in the safe operation of tools that were traditionally found only in specialized workshops or engineering labs. Many libraries manage this by asking visitors to complete a short safety orientation before using higher-risk equipment for the first time.

Even with these challenges, most library systems report that makerspaces are consistently busy, particularly after school and on weekends. Supporters argue that makerspaces help close a gap between students whose families can afford expensive hobby equipment and students who cannot, giving every visitor, regardless of income, a chance to build, design, and create. As demand continues to grow, several Ontario library systems have announced plans to open additional makerspace locations in the coming years.

Passage information_paragraph_198

Many Ontario high schools have introduced recycling and composting programs designed to reduce the amount of waste sent to landfills each year. These programs typically place labelled bins for garbage, recycling, and organic waste, such as food scraps, in cafeterias, hallways, and classrooms, encouraging students to sort their waste correctly before throwing it away. Unlike a simple recycling bin placed in a hallway, a full composting program usually requires a school to arrange separate pickup for organic waste and to train students and staff on which items belong in which bin.

Before these programs existed, most school waste, including food scraps that could have been composted, was combined into a single garbage stream and sent directly to a landfill. Landfills produce methane gas as organic waste breaks down without oxygen, and methane is a greenhouse gas that contributes to climate change. By diverting food scraps to composting instead, schools can reduce the amount of methane produced while also creating nutrient-rich compost that can be used in school gardens or donated to local farms.

Starting a composting program is rarely simple. Schools must first educate students and staff about what actually belongs in each bin, since items like compostable cutlery or coated paper cups often confuse people who assume they can be composted or recycled when they cannot. Many schools address this by forming a student-led environmental club that monitors bins during lunch periods, correcting mistakes and answering questions from classmates who are unsure where a particular item belongs.

The cost of running these programs varies depending on whether a municipality already offers organic waste pickup for schools or whether the school must arrange and pay for its own service. Some school boards have found that reducing regular garbage pickup, since less waste needs to go to a landfill once composting begins, helps offset the added cost of organic waste collection. Grants from environmental organizations and provincial government programs have also helped several schools cover the upfront cost of new bins and educational signage.

Students who take part in these programs often report a stronger awareness of how much waste a single school produces in a day, and many say the experience changes their habits outside of school as well. Environmental educators note that hands-on programs like composting tend to be more effective at teaching sustainability than classroom lessons alone, since students see the direct results of their sorting decisions. As more schools adopt these programs, some school boards are now setting formal waste-reduction targets, aiming to divert a specific percentage of total waste away from landfills within the next several years.

Passage information_paragraph_204

A growing number of Ontario teens are taking on part-time jobs while still in high school, working in places like grocery stores, restaurants, retail shops, and summer camps. Ontario's employment standards allow teens as young as fourteen to work in many jobs, though certain restrictions apply to younger workers, including limits on how late they can work on a school night and which industries, such as construction, they are not permitted to enter until they are older.

For many teens, a part-time job provides more than just spending money. Researchers who study youth employment have found that teens who work moderate hours, generally under fifteen to twenty hours per week during the school year, often develop strong time-management skills, since they must balance schoolwork, extracurricular activities, and job shifts within the same week. Employers frequently note that teenage workers gain confidence in dealing directly with customers, handling money, and following through on responsibilities without constant supervision from a parent or teacher.

Choosing the right part-time job often depends on a teen's schedule and interests. A student involved in a competitive sport might look for a job with flexible scheduling, such as a retail store that can adjust shifts around practice times, while a student interested in a future career in healthcare might seek a volunteer or part-time role at a local pharmacy or clinic instead. Some schools now offer co-op programs that combine part-time work experience directly with course credit, allowing students to explore a career field while still completing their high school diploma.

Part-time work is not without its downsides. Education experts caution that working too many hours, particularly during exam periods, can negatively affect a student's grades and overall well-being. A small number of studies have found that students working more than twenty hours per week during the school year are more likely to report high stress levels and reduced sleep compared to students who work fewer hours or none at all. Because of this, many parents and guidance counsellors recommend that teens carefully track their weekly hours and communicate early with employers if a job begins interfering with schoolwork.

Despite these concerns, most career counsellors agree that a part-time job, taken on in moderation, offers real long-term value. Beyond the paycheque, a first job often becomes a teen's introduction to workplace expectations such as punctuality, teamwork, and professional communication, all skills that transfer directly into future full-time employment. Many adults, when asked about their own first part-time job, describe it as one of the more formative experiences of their teenage years, shaping habits that stayed with them well beyond high school.

Passage information_paragraph_210

Battle of the bands competitions have become a popular way for Ontario high schools to showcase student musicians, giving young performers a real stage and audience beyond the school talent show. In a typical battle of the bands, several student groups each perform a short set, usually between three and five songs, in front of a panel of judges and a live audience made up of classmates, teachers, and family members. Judges typically score performances on categories such as musicianship, stage presence, originality, and how well the group works together as a unit.

These competitions usually begin months before the actual event, as student bands form, choose a name, and begin rehearsing in whatever space is available, whether that is a garage, a school music room, or a friend's basement. Many schools require bands to submit a recorded audition before being accepted into the competition, partly to manage how many groups can realistically perform in one evening and partly to ensure sound quality meets a basic standard. Organizing teachers, often from the music or drama department, coordinate logistics such as sound equipment, stage scheduling, and ticket sales, frequently with help from a dedicated group of student volunteers.

For many students, a battle of the bands is their first experience performing in front of a large audience outside of a formal school concert. Unlike a school concert, where students typically perform pieces selected or arranged by a teacher, a battle of the bands usually allows performers to choose their own songs, including original compositions written by band members themselves. This creative freedom is part of what makes the events popular, since audiences often get to hear original music alongside covers of well-known songs.

Money raised from ticket sales at these events is frequently donated to a cause chosen by the school, such as a local music program, a charity, or an initiative to purchase new instruments for students who cannot afford their own. Some schools have used battle of the bands proceeds to fund an entire year of after-school music lessons for students who otherwise could not access private instruction. This connection between the competition and a charitable goal often increases attendance, since students and families feel their ticket purchase supports something beyond a single evening's entertainment.

While the competitive element draws attention, many participating students say the greatest value comes from the experience itself: writing original material, learning to work as part of a group under pressure, and performing live in front of an unpredictable crowd. Music teachers who organize these events often note that even bands who do not place among the top finishers describe the process as one of the most rewarding parts of their high school experience, citing the friendships and confidence built through weeks of shared rehearsal.

Passage information_paragraph_216

Cities across Ontario have been adding dedicated bike lanes to major streets, aiming to give cyclists a safer, more predictable space to travel separate from car traffic. A bike lane is a section of roadway reserved specifically for bicycles, sometimes marked only with painted lines and sometimes physically separated from car traffic by a raised curb, planters, or flexible posts. City planners generally agree that physically separated bike lanes, often called protected bike lanes, provide a greater sense of safety for cyclists than lanes marked with paint alone.

The push for more bike lanes has grown alongside a broader effort by many municipalities to reduce car traffic, lower greenhouse gas emissions, and encourage residents to choose walking or cycling for short trips instead of driving. Traffic studies conducted in several Ontario cities have found that streets with protected bike lanes tend to see fewer serious collisions involving cyclists, since drivers and cyclists have clearly separated space rather than sharing a single lane.

Adding new bike lanes is rarely a simple process. City councils must weigh the benefits of improved cyclist safety against concerns raised by some residents and business owners, who worry that removing a lane of car traffic or on-street parking to make room for a bike lane could hurt local businesses or increase congestion for drivers. Public consultations, where residents can share their opinions before a project moves forward, have become a standard part of the process in most Ontario municipalities considering new bike infrastructure.

For teens who are old enough to cycle independently to school, part-time jobs, or friends' houses, protected bike lanes can make a real difference in whether a parent feels comfortable allowing that independence. A teen commuting along a busy arterial road without any bike lane must share space directly with fast-moving vehicles, while the same trip along a street with a protected bike lane offers a clearly marked, separated path. Several school boards have noted an increase in students cycling to school after nearby streets received new bike infrastructure, particularly on routes that previously required crossing multiple lanes of car traffic.

Despite ongoing debate over specific projects, most major Ontario cities have committed to long-term cycling network plans that aim to connect neighbourhoods with continuous, protected routes rather than short, disconnected segments. Transportation planners argue that a connected network matters more than any single lane, since a cyclist who feels safe on one street but must merge into traffic a block later gains little real benefit. As these networks slowly expand, city staff continue to track collision data and cyclist counts to determine which types of bike lanes work best in different neighbourhoods.

Passage information_paragraph_222

Health researchers have long pointed out that most teenagers do not get enough sleep, and Ontario schools, health units, and family doctors have increasingly focused attention on why this gap between recommended and actual sleep matters so much during the teenage years. Sleep experts generally recommend that teens between the ages of thirteen and eighteen get between eight and ten hours of sleep each night, yet surveys of Canadian high school students consistently find that a large percentage report averaging closer to six or seven hours on a typical school night.

Part of the explanation lies in a natural shift in the body's internal clock that occurs during adolescence. Researchers have found that puberty causes the release of the sleep hormone melatonin to happen later in the evening than it did during childhood, making it biologically more difficult for many teens to feel tired before eleven o'clock at night, even when they know an early alarm is coming. At the same time, most Ontario high schools still start around eight or eight thirty in the morning, a schedule set decades ago that many sleep researchers argue no longer matches what is now understood about the teenage body clock.

The consequences of chronic sleep shortage go beyond simply feeling tired. Studies connect insufficient sleep in teens to reduced concentration in class, slower reaction times, a weaker immune system, and a higher risk of mood difficulties such as increased irritability or anxiety. Coaches and athletic trainers have also pointed out that athletes who are sleep deprived are more likely to suffer sports injuries, since fatigue slows reaction time and impairs coordination during physical activity.

Some school boards, both in Canada and internationally, have experimented with shifting high school start times later in the morning to better match the teenage sleep cycle. Early results from schools that delayed their start time by even thirty minutes have shown modest improvements in student attendance, reported alertness, and in some cases even academic performance. However, changing a school's start time affects far more than students alone, since bus schedules, after-school jobs, and extracurricular practices are all built around the existing timetable, making widespread change slow and complicated to implement.

In the meantime, health professionals encourage teens to build habits that support better sleep even within an early school schedule, such as limiting screen use in the hour before bed, keeping a consistent sleep and wake time on weekends as well as weekdays, and avoiding caffeine later in the day. While no single change guarantees perfect sleep, experts agree that small, consistent habits, combined with growing public awareness of the biological reasons behind teen sleep struggles, can meaningfully improve how rested and focused a student feels during the school day.

  3. This response links a real personal experience to the passage's point about reduced concentration from insufficient sleep. The other options dismiss the topic instead of connecting to a specific detail.
  4. The correct answer is A (They are more likely to suffer sports injuries due to slower reaction time and impaired coordination): They are more likely to suffer sports injuries due to slower reaction time and impaired coordination. The passage states this finding directly, linking fatigue to a higher injury risk during physical activity. Options B, C, and D contradict or are not supported by the passage.
  5. The correct answer is D (continuing or persisting over a long period): continuing or persisting over a long period. The passage uses "chronic sleep shortage" to describe an ongoing pattern of insufficient sleep rather than a single bad night. Options A, B, and C describe meanings that do not match how the word is used.

Passage information_paragraph_228

Photography has become one of the most accessible creative hobbies available to teenagers, largely because most smartphones now include cameras capable of producing images that once required expensive, specialized equipment. Unlike hobbies that require a significant upfront investment, such as musical instruments or sports equipment, many teens can begin experimenting with photography using a device they already own, learning the basics of composition, lighting, and editing before ever purchasing a dedicated camera.

Interest in photography as a structured hobby has grown alongside the popularity of photo-sharing platforms, where users post images to a wide audience and often receive feedback in the form of comments or reactions. While casual snapshots make up much of what gets shared online, a growing number of teens have moved toward more deliberate photography, studying concepts such as the rule of thirds, which suggests placing a subject off-centre within an image, or golden hour lighting, the warm, soft light that occurs shortly after sunrise or before sunset.

Some Ontario high schools now offer photography as a credit course, giving students access to equipment such as digital cameras, tripods, and photo-editing software that many families could not otherwise afford. These courses typically combine technical instruction, such as understanding aperture and shutter speed, with creative assignments that ask students to tell a story or capture a specific mood through a single image or a small series of photographs. Teachers who run these programs often note that photography appeals to students who may not feel drawn to traditional visual art forms like painting or drawing but still want a creative outlet.

Beyond the classroom, many teens pursue photography informally, documenting school events, sports games, or community gatherings, sometimes building a small local reputation that leads to informal paid work, such as photographing a friend's birthday party or a local sports team's season. A few motivated students have used a growing photography portfolio to apply for part-time work at local newspapers, wedding photography businesses, or community organizations that need someone to document events on a regular basis.

Like any skill, photography improves with consistent practice rather than expensive equipment alone. Experienced photographers frequently advise beginners to take photos regularly, review their own work critically, and study images they admire to understand why those images work, rather than assuming a better camera alone will produce better results. For many teens, photography also offers something less measurable than technical skill: a habit of noticing small, interesting details in everyday surroundings that might otherwise go completely unnoticed, whether that is the way light falls across a hallway or the exact moment a friend bursts into genuine laughter.

Passage information_paragraph_234

Every spring, when the days start to warm up but the nights are still cold, maple syrup producers across Ontario begin one of the province's oldest traditions. Making maple syrup is a process that combines careful timing, simple tools, and a lot of patience, and it turns a clear, watery liquid into the sweet syrup found on breakfast tables everywhere.

The process begins with tapping the trees. Producers drill a small hole into the trunk of a sugar maple and insert a metal spout called a spile. A bucket or a length of plastic tubing is attached to the spile to collect the sap as it drips out. A single tree can produce anywhere from twenty to fifty litres of sap over the course of a season, depending on the size of the tree and the weather.

Timing matters a great deal in this industry. Sap only flows well when daytime temperatures rise above freezing while nighttime temperatures drop back below zero. This freeze-thaw cycle creates pressure changes inside the tree that push the sap out through the tap hole. If the weather stays too warm or too cold for several days in a row, the sap stops flowing and producers have to wait for better conditions.

Once collected, the raw sap looks almost exactly like water and tastes only faintly sweet. It has to be boiled for a long time to remove most of the water and concentrate the natural sugars. Producers use a piece of equipment called an evaporator, a shallow pan set over a steady heat source, to boil large volumes of sap efficiently. It can take about forty litres of raw sap to produce just one litre of finished syrup, which explains why pure maple syrup costs more than many other sweeteners.

As the sap boils down, workers watch closely for changes in colour, density, and temperature. When the liquid reaches the correct concentration, usually measured with a hydrometer or by checking the boiling point, it is filtered to remove tiny mineral particles and then graded by colour and flavour before being bottled.

Today, some larger operations use modern technology such as vacuum tubing systems and reverse osmosis machines to speed up collection and reduce boiling time, while smaller family-run sugar bushes often still rely on methods that have changed very little over the past century. Either way, the finished product connects modern kitchens to a practice that Indigenous peoples in the region were using long before European settlers arrived.

Option A and C describe steady temperatures that would not create the pressure changes needed to push sap out. Option D describes rain and sunshine, which the passage never mentions as a relevant factor in sap flow.

Options A, B, and C all give smaller amounts that are not supported anywhere in the passage. This large ratio is also the exact reason the passage gives for why pure maple syrup costs more than many other sweeteners.
  3. Option B is not supported, since the passage never claims maple trees are rare. Options C and D introduce taxes and imports, neither of which the passage mentions at all.
  4. Option A is too extreme, since traditional methods clearly still exist. Options B and D both introduce claims about safety and taste that the passage never makes.

Option A involves an unrelated topic that offers no real personal connection. Options C and D relate to maple syrup only loosely, through recipes or pricing, rather than the patience the actual production process demands.

Passage information_paragraph_240

Over the past two decades, cities and towns across Ontario have added skateboard parks to their list of public recreation facilities, alongside soccer fields, splash pads, and basketball courts. What began as a niche interest for a small group of skateboarding enthusiasts has grown into a mainstream form of recreation that many municipalities now actively plan for.

A skateboard park, sometimes called a skate park, is an outdoor or indoor space built specifically for skateboarding, inline skating, and often BMX biking. Most parks include a mix of features such as ramps, rails, ledges, and bowls, which are curved sections that let skaters build up speed and perform tricks. Some parks are designed in a style called street, which imitates the concrete stairs, curbs, and ledges found in an urban downtown, while others use a bowl or park style with smooth, flowing transitions similar to an empty swimming pool.

Municipal planners consider several factors before building a skate park. Location matters because the park needs to be far enough from homes that the sound of wheels on concrete does not disturb residents, yet close enough to public transit and other recreation areas that young people can reach it easily. Surfacing is another major consideration, since a smooth, properly poured concrete surface reduces injuries and lasts far longer than asphalt.

Supporters of skate parks often point to benefits beyond simple recreation. Because skateboarding requires balance, coordination, and repeated practice to master even basic tricks, many young people develop patience and resilience through the sport. Skate parks also give teenagers a free, structured place to spend time with friends, which some community organizations argue helps reduce loitering in areas like parking lots or empty lots that are not designed for recreation.

Building a skate park is not without challenges. Construction and ongoing maintenance can be costly, since concrete surfaces eventually crack and need repair, and municipalities must decide whether to hire skate park design specialists or general contractors who may be less familiar with the sport's specific needs. Some communities have overcome funding gaps by combining municipal budgets with grants from provincial recreation programs and fundraising efforts led by local skateboarding clubs.

Today, many Ontario skate parks host community events, from casual weekend meetups to organized competitions that draw skaters from across the region, showing how a once-overlooked hobby has become a valued part of local recreation planning.

Passage information_paragraph_246

Three-dimensional printing, often shortened to 3D printing, is a manufacturing process that builds solid objects layer by layer from a digital design. Unlike traditional manufacturing, which often starts with a large block of material and cuts away what is not needed, 3D printing adds material only where it is required, which is why the process is sometimes called additive manufacturing.

The process begins with a digital model created using computer-aided design software. This software allows a designer to build a three-dimensional shape on a screen, adjusting its size, angles, and internal structure before anything is physically produced. Once the design is finished, specialized slicing software converts the model into thin horizontal layers and generates precise instructions that tell the printer exactly where to place material for each layer.

Most 3D printers used in schools and homes rely on a method called fused deposition modelling. In this method, a spool of plastic filament is fed through a heated nozzle, which melts the plastic just enough to extrude it in a thin, controlled stream. The printer's nozzle moves back and forth across a build platform, tracing the outline of each layer according to the sliced instructions. Once one layer is complete, the platform lowers slightly, and the nozzle begins printing the next layer directly on top. This process repeats, sometimes for hours, until the final object is complete.

Other types of 3D printers use different materials and techniques. Some professional printers use a liquid resin that hardens when exposed to ultraviolet light, producing objects with a much smoother surface than filament-based printing can achieve. Industrial printers can even use powdered metal, fusing tiny particles together with a laser to create strong, precise parts used in fields such as aerospace and medicine.

3D printing has become useful across many industries because it allows designers to create prototypes quickly and cheaply compared to traditional manufacturing methods, which often require expensive moulds or tools before a single object can be produced. Engineers can test a design, identify flaws, and print a revised version within the same day rather than waiting weeks for a redesigned part to arrive.

Despite these advantages, 3D printing still has limitations. Printing large or highly detailed objects can take many hours, and the strength of a printed object depends heavily on the material used and how the layers are oriented. As the technology continues to improve, however, 3D printers are becoming faster, more affordable, and capable of working with an increasing variety of materials.

  1. Option A makes a claim about weight that the passage never supports. Options B and C both describe software and colouring, neither of which relates to why the process is called additive.

Option A describes carving, which is the opposite approach the passage contrasts 3D printing against. Options B and D describe resin and powdered metal printers, which the passage identifies as separate, different types of printers.

Option A is contradicted, since computer-aided design software is described as the first essential step. Options C and D make overstated claims about strength and flaw detection that the passage never actually makes.

Option B describes a specific material used only in industrial printers, not a repeated-step process. Options C and D describe software and hardening methods rather than the repeated, layer-by-layer building process.

Passage information_paragraph_252

In neighbourhoods across Ontario, empty lots and unused patches of park land are increasingly being transformed into community gardens, shared spaces where residents can grow vegetables, fruits, and flowers together. These gardens have become popular not only because they produce fresh food, but also because of the social and environmental benefits they bring to the people who use them.

A community garden is typically divided into individual plots that are assigned to different families or organized as one large shared space that everyone tends together. Local governments, schools, and non-profit organizations often manage the land, setting basic rules about watering schedules, pathway maintenance, and what can be planted. Many gardens also include a communal compost bin, where food scraps and plant waste are collected and eventually turned into nutrient-rich soil that gardeners can use.

One of the clearest benefits of community gardens is access to fresh, affordable produce. Families who might not otherwise be able to afford a steady supply of fruits and vegetables can grow their own, reducing grocery costs and improving their diet at the same time. In some neighbourhoods, gardens with extra produce donate surplus vegetables to local food banks, extending the benefit beyond the gardeners themselves.

Community gardens also create opportunities for neighbours to meet and build relationships they might not otherwise form. Working side by side while weeding, watering, or harvesting gives people a shared purpose, and many gardeners report feeling more connected to their neighbourhood after joining. For newcomers to a community, a garden can be an especially welcoming space to practise a new language, share recipes, and learn about local growing seasons from more experienced gardeners.

Environmental benefits are another important part of the picture. Gardens can reduce the amount of pavement and unused grass in a neighbourhood, which helps rainwater soak into the ground instead of running off into storm drains. Growing food locally also reduces the distance produce has to travel before reaching a plate, which can lower the transportation emissions associated with large-scale farming and shipping.

Starting and maintaining a community garden does come with challenges. Someone needs to organize volunteers, secure a reliable water source, and resolve occasional disagreements over shared responsibilities or plot boundaries. Despite these hurdles, many cities continue to support new garden projects because the combination of fresh food, stronger neighbourhood connections, and environmental benefits makes the effort worthwhile for the communities involved.

Passage information_paragraph_258

Service dogs play an important role in helping people with disabilities live more independently, performing tasks ranging from guiding someone who is blind to alerting a person with diabetes when their blood sugar drops too low. Training a dog to perform these tasks reliably takes years of consistent work and begins long before the dog is paired with the person it will eventually assist.

Most service dog organizations start the training process when a puppy is only a few weeks old. During this early stage, puppies are exposed to a wide variety of sounds, surfaces, and situations, such as crowded sidewalks, elevators, and busy stores, so they become comfortable in environments they will later need to work in calmly. This stage, often called socialization, is considered one of the most important parts of training because a dog that is easily startled or distracted cannot safely perform its future duties.

Around the age of one, many puppies move on to formal task training, where they learn specific skills related to the type of assistance they will eventually provide. A guide dog for someone who is blind, for example, learns to stop at curbs, navigate around obstacles, and even disobey a command if following it would put its handler in danger, a skill trainers call intelligent disobedience. A medical alert dog, by contrast, is trained to recognize subtle changes in a person's scent or behaviour that signal a medical emergency, such as a seizure or a dangerous drop in blood sugar.

Training a service dog is a lengthy process, often taking between eighteen months and two years before the dog is ready to be matched with a handler. Even after that point, the dog and its new handler typically spend several weeks training together so the dog can learn the specific routines, environments, and needs of the person it will be working with every day.

Not every dog that begins training successfully becomes a certified service dog. Some dogs are too easily distracted, too anxious in new situations, or simply better suited to being a pet than a working animal. Dogs that do not complete the program are often adopted into loving homes, sometimes by the very volunteers who helped raise and train them.

Because of the time, expertise, and resources required, training a single service dog can cost tens of thousands of dollars, which is why many organizations rely heavily on donations, fundraising events, and volunteer puppy raisers to keep their programs running.

  1. The correct answer is C (To help puppies become comfortable in a wide variety of environments): The passage explains that socialization exposes puppies to different sounds, surfaces, and situations so they become comfortable working calmly later on. Option A describes advanced task training, which happens only after socialization. Options B and D both misstate the purpose of this early stage, which focuses on comfort rather than matching or disobedience.

Option A misrepresents the concept as random or mood-based disobedience. Options B and C describe unrelated behaviours, such as barking or refusing to work, that the passage never connects to this term.
  3. The correct answer is A (Success in the program depends on a dog's temperament and focus): The passage explains that some dogs are too easily distracted, too anxious, or better suited to being a pet, all of which point to temperament as the deciding factor. Option B invents a fixed quota that the passage never mentions. Options C and D both introduce reasons, health problems and randomness, that contradict the temperament-based explanation the passage actually gives.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

Passage information_paragraph_264

Every autumn, the leaves on deciduous trees across Ontario shift from green to brilliant shades of yellow, orange, and red before eventually falling to the ground. While this seasonal change is often admired simply for its beauty, it is actually the result of a precise biological process that helps trees survive the colder months ahead.

During the spring and summer, leaves appear green because of a pigment called chlorophyll, which absorbs sunlight and uses its energy to convert water and carbon dioxide into sugars the tree can use for growth. This process, known as photosynthesis, requires warm temperatures and long hours of daylight to work efficiently. Chlorophyll is so abundant in a healthy leaf during these months that it masks other pigments that are present in the leaf the entire time.

As autumn approaches and daylight hours shorten, trees begin preparing for winter by shutting down photosynthesis in their leaves. A layer of specialized cells forms at the base of each leaf stem, gradually blocking the flow of water and nutrients between the leaf and the rest of the tree. Without a steady supply of resources, the leaf can no longer produce new chlorophyll, and the green pigment already present slowly breaks down.

As chlorophyll fades, other pigments that were hidden underneath become visible. Yellow and orange colours come from pigments called carotenoids, which are present in the leaf throughout the growing season but simply overshadowed by chlorophyll until autumn arrives. Red and purple colours, on the other hand, come from a different pigment called anthocyanin, which some trees actually produce in larger amounts during autumn, often triggered by bright sunny days combined with cool nights.

The exact colours and intensity of a given autumn vary from year to year depending on weather conditions. A growing season with plenty of rain followed by a dry, sunny autumn with cool but not freezing nights tends to produce the most vivid red and orange displays. In contrast, a fall with early frosts, heavy winds, or extended cloudy periods can cause leaves to turn brown and drop earlier without ever developing bright colours.

Eventually, the layer of cells at the base of the stem finishes sealing off the leaf completely, and it detaches from the branch. This process protects the tree from losing water through its leaves during winter, when frozen ground makes it difficult for roots to absorb moisture.

  1. The correct answer is B (A pigment called chlorophyll that absorbs sunlight for photosynthesis): The passage states directly that leaves appear green because of chlorophyll, which absorbs sunlight to power photosynthesis. Option A describes a structure involved in autumn leaf drop, not summer colour. Options C and D both describe autumn conditions that are unrelated to why leaves are green during the growing season.

Option B incorrectly describes an increase in daylight, which is the opposite of what actually happens in autumn. Options A and C both describe carbon dioxide and root water absorption, neither of which the passage names as the trigger.
  3. The correct answer is D (The abundant chlorophyll in summer leaves masks the carotenoids present): The passage states that carotenoids are present throughout the growing season but are simply overshadowed by the abundant chlorophyll until autumn. Option A directly contradicts this, since carotenoids are present the entire time. Options B and C both describe production stopping or colours appearing only after falling, neither of which the passage supports.
  4. The correct answer is A (An autumn with early frosts, strong winds, or long cloudy periods): The passage states directly that these conditions can cause leaves to turn brown and drop earlier without developing bright colours. Option B actually describes the ideal conditions for vivid colour, the opposite of dull, early-falling leaves. Options C and D describe spring rain and slower daylight change, neither of which the passage links to dull, early leaf drop.
  5. The correct answer is C (Noticing how the leaves on a favourite tree gradually change colour each year): This experience directly connects to the passage's focus on the seasonal colour change happening in real, familiar trees each autumn. Option A discusses unrelated ocean plant pigments rather than autumn leaves. Options B and D describe rainfall data and animal migration, both unrelated to the specific process of leaves changing colour.
  6. The correct answer is B (It seals off the leaf completely, detaching it from the branch): The passage states that the layer of cells at the base of the stem eventually finishes sealing off the leaf completely, causing it to detach. Option A describes extra water flow, which contradicts the passage's description of water being blocked, not increased. Options C and D both describe sunlight reflection and carbon dioxide absorption, neither of which the passage mentions as the cause of leaf drop.
Passage information_paragraph_270

Recycling programs have become a standard part of daily life in Ontario schools, with colour-coded bins for paper, containers, and organic waste appearing in cafeterias, hallways, and classrooms across the province. While students often use these bins without thinking twice, the process of sorting and processing school waste involves several steps that most people never see.

Inside a typical school, recycling usually begins with three separate bins: one for paper and cardboard, one for plastic and metal containers, and one for organic waste such as food scraps and compostable packaging. Custodial staff or student volunteers empty these bins regularly, keeping the different materials separate so they do not become contaminated with items that do not belong. Contamination is a serious concern because a small amount of food waste mixed into a bin of paper can ruin an entire batch, forcing the whole bin to be sent to a landfill instead of a recycling facility.

Once collected, recyclable materials from a school are usually picked up by the same municipal waste service that serves nearby homes and businesses. At a materials recovery facility, machines and workers sort the mixed recyclables by material type, using magnets to pull out metal objects and optical scanners to separate different types of plastic. Paper and cardboard are baled into large blocks and shipped to mills, where they are broken down into pulp and turned into new paper products.

Organic waste follows a different path. Food scraps and compostable materials collected from schools are often sent to a composting facility, where they are combined with other organic waste and left to break down over several weeks or months. Workers monitor moisture and temperature levels throughout this process to speed up decomposition and prevent unpleasant odours. The finished compost is a nutrient-rich material that is often sold or given to farms, landscaping companies, or community gardens.

Many schools go beyond simply providing bins by running education programs that teach students how to sort waste correctly. Some schools appoint green teams of student volunteers who monitor bins during lunch periods, gently correcting classmates who place items in the wrong container. Research has shown that schools with active green teams tend to have significantly lower contamination rates than schools that rely on signage alone.

Even with these programs in place, recycling in schools is not perfect. Busy lunch periods, unclear labelling, and simple mistakes mean that some recyclable material still ends up in the garbage, which is why ongoing education remains an important part of any school recycling program.

  1. The correct answer is B (Even a small amount of food waste can ruin a whole bin of paper): The passage states directly that a small amount of food waste mixed into a paper bin can ruin the entire batch, sending it to a landfill instead. Passage information_paragraph_276

Every September, millions of people across Canada and around the world take part in the Terry Fox Run, an event that raises money for cancer research. The run has grown into one of the largest fundraising events for a single cause anywhere in the world, but its origins trace back to one young man's personal journey.

Terry Fox was a Canadian athlete who lost part of his right leg to bone cancer when he was eighteen years old. While recovering, he decided to run across Canada to raise money and awareness for cancer research, an effort he called the Marathon of Hope. In April 1980, he dipped his artificial leg into the Atlantic Ocean in Newfoundland and began running westward, aiming to reach the Pacific Ocean on the other side of the country.

Running roughly the distance of a marathon almost every single day, Terry Fox made his way through several provinces over the following months, drawing growing crowds and media attention as word of his journey spread. However, after one hundred and forty-three days and more than five thousand kilometres, he was forced to stop running near Thunder Bay, Ontario, when doctors discovered that his cancer had spread to his lungs. He passed away the following year, but his goal of raising awareness and funds for cancer research did not end with him.

Just a year after his run was cut short, the first organized Terry Fox Run was held to continue his fundraising mission, and it has taken place every September since then. Unlike a competitive race, the Terry Fox Run has no winners or official finish times. Participants can walk, run, cycle, or roll in a wheelchair, covering any distance they choose, from a short walk around a schoolyard to a longer route through a local park.

Schools across Ontario play a major role in keeping the tradition alive, often organizing their own Terry Fox Run each fall as one of the first community events of the school year. Students learn about Terry Fox's story in class before taking part, connecting the physical activity to the history and purpose behind it. Money raised through school events, along with community runs held in cities and towns nationwide, is donated to the Terry Fox Foundation, which funds cancer research projects across the country.

Decades after his run ended, Terry Fox remains one of the most recognized Canadian figures in history, and the annual event named after him continues to remind participants that a single person's determination can create lasting change.

Passage information_paragraph_282

Competitive video gaming, commonly known as esports, has grown from a casual hobby into a structured activity with organized teams, coaches, and tournaments that can draw audiences of millions. High schools and universities across Ontario have started forming their own esports teams, treating the activity much like a traditional sport in terms of practice schedules and teamwork.

Most esports teams focus on a specific game, such as a strategy game, a team-based shooter, or a sports simulation, since the skills and teamwork required can differ significantly between game types. A typical team is made up of several players who each take on a specific role, similar to positions on a soccer or basketball team. In many strategy and shooter games, players must communicate constantly during a match, calling out enemy positions, coordinating attacks, and adjusting strategy based on what is happening on screen.

Practice sessions for competitive esports teams look different from casual gaming but share some similarities with traditional sports training. Teams often begin with individual warm-up exercises designed to sharpen reaction time and precision, followed by scrimmages, which are practice matches against another team. Coaches review recordings of these scrimmages afterward, pointing out mistakes in positioning, communication, or decision-making, much like a coach reviewing game footage in football or hockey.

Physical fitness has become an increasingly important part of esports training as well. Long practice sessions require players to sit in the same position for hours, which can lead to issues such as eye strain, wrist injuries, and poor posture if not managed carefully. Many competitive teams now include stretching routines, scheduled breaks, and even basic cardiovascular exercise as part of a player's regular training plan, since better physical health has been linked to sharper focus and faster reaction times during long matches.

Tournaments for school and university esports teams typically take place online, with matches streamed live so classmates and family members can watch from anywhere. Some larger tournaments are held in person at dedicated venues, complete with commentators and large screens for spectators, closely resembling the atmosphere of a traditional sports championship.

As esports continues to gain recognition, some Ontario schools have begun offering scholarships or course credit for students who participate seriously, treating competitive gaming as a legitimate extracurricular activity worth the same structure, discipline, and support given to more traditional school sports.

Passage information_paragraph_288

As concerns about climate change and rising energy costs continue to grow, solar panels have become an increasingly common sight on rooftops across Ontario. While most people know that solar panels somehow turn sunlight into electricity, the actual science behind the process involves a fascinating interaction between light and specially designed materials.

Solar panels are made up of many individual units called solar cells, most of which are constructed from a material called silicon. Silicon is a semiconductor, meaning it can control the flow of electricity better than a material like rubber, which blocks electricity entirely, but not as freely as a material like copper, which conducts electricity easily. This unique property makes silicon ideal for capturing and directing the energy carried by sunlight.

When sunlight strikes a solar cell, tiny packets of light energy called photons hit the silicon and transfer their energy to electrons within the material. This transfer of energy knocks the electrons loose, allowing them to move freely rather than staying fixed in place. Solar cells are specially designed with two layers of silicon that have been treated with different chemical elements, creating an internal electric field that pushes the loosened electrons in one consistent direction rather than letting them move randomly.

This organized movement of electrons creates an electric current, which flows out of the solar cell through metal contacts and wires. However, the electricity produced at this stage is direct current, the same type of electricity found in a battery, while most homes and the electrical grid run on alternating current. To solve this mismatch, solar panel systems include a device called an inverter, which converts the direct current produced by the panels into the alternating current that household appliances can actually use.

The amount of electricity a solar panel system produces depends on several factors, including the angle and direction the panels face, the amount of direct sunlight they receive throughout the day, and local weather conditions such as cloud cover. Panels installed on a south-facing roof in an area with long summer days typically generate significantly more electricity than panels installed in a shaded or poorly angled location.

Many homeowners who install solar panels also add a system for storing excess electricity in batteries or sending it back to the electrical grid, allowing them to use stored or credited energy during cloudy days or at night when their panels are not actively producing power.

  1. The correct answer is B (Silicon is a semiconductor that can control the flow of electricity): The passage explains directly that silicon is a semiconductor, controlling electricity better than rubber while conducting less freely than copper. Option A incorrectly claims silicon conducts exactly like copper at all times. Options C and D both misrepresent silicon as fully blocking electricity or changing colour, neither of which the passage describes.
  2. The correct answer is D (It converts direct current from panels into usable alternating current): The passage states this exact function, explaining that the inverter converts the direct current from the panels into the alternating current homes actually use. Option A describes battery storage, which is a separate system the passage discusses afterward. Options B and C both describe cleaning or angling functions that the passage never connects to an inverter.
  3. The correct answer is D (More direct sunlight exposure allows panels to produce more electricity): The passage explains that panels on a south-facing roof with long summer days typically generate more electricity, directly linking sunlight exposure to output. Option A invents a legal requirement about panel size that the passage never mentions. Options B and C both make overstated claims about shaded roofs or exclusive southern orientation that the passage does not support.
  4. The correct answer is B (Charging a phone battery during the day to use it again at night): This everyday experience closely mirrors the passage's description of storing excess solar electricity in batteries for later use during cloudy days or at night. Option A discusses unrelated historical background rather than a personal storage experience. Options C and D both describe pricing comparisons or mining processes that are unrelated to the concept of storing energy for later.
  5. The correct answer is A (They transfer energy to electrons, knocking them loose to move freely): The passage states directly that photons transfer their energy to electrons, knocking them loose so they can move freely within the material. Option B incorrectly skips ahead to the role of the inverter, which happens much later in the process. Options C and D both describe melting or permanent storage that the passage never mentions as part of this step.
  6. Option A invents a legal requirement that the passage never mentions. Options B and D both describe functions, current conversion or increased sunlight absorption, that the passage already attributes to other parts of the system.
Passage information_paragraph_294

Across Ontario, community gardens have become a familiar sight in parks, schoolyards, and empty lots. A community garden is a shared piece of land where neighbours grow vegetables, herbs, and flowers together instead of gardening alone in separate backyards. These gardens are usually divided into small plots that residents can rent for a low yearly fee, and some spaces are left open for anyone to tend as a group.

The idea behind community gardens is simple, but the benefits reach far beyond fresh produce. Neighbours who might never have spoken to each other end up trading tips about tomatoes, sharing tools, and helping water each other's plants when someone goes on vacation. For newcomers to a neighbourhood, a garden plot can be one of the fastest ways to meet people and feel like part of a community.

Community gardens also address a real problem in many cities: access to affordable, healthy food. In some neighbourhoods, the nearest grocery store with fresh produce is a long bus ride away, while convenience stores nearby mostly sell packaged snacks. A garden plot lets a family grow its own carrots, beans, and peppers for a fraction of the store price, and many gardens donate extra produce to local food banks at the end of the season.

Running a garden takes more coordination than most people expect. Someone has to arrange for water access, decide how plots are assigned, settle disagreements over shared paths, and organize a spring clean-up before planting begins. Many gardens form a small volunteer committee to handle these tasks, and new gardeners are often paired with an experienced member who can answer questions about soil, pests, and when to plant.

Schools have started to notice the value of gardening too. Several Ontario schools now maintain their own gardens where students learn about plant biology, measure growth over the semester, and sometimes cook with what they harvest. Teachers say that students who help grow a vegetable are far more likely to actually taste it, even if they were picky eaters before.

Not every gardener sees results right away. A first-time gardener might lose a crop to an early frost or a determined groundhog, and the learning curve can be frustrating. Experienced gardeners generally agree that patience is the most important skill, since a garden rewards steady attention over months rather than a single afternoon of effort. For many participants, though, the wait is part of what makes the eventual harvest so satisfying.

Passage information_paragraph_300

Skateboarding has grown from a niche hobby into one of the most popular activities among Canadian teenagers, and many cities have responded by building dedicated skate parks. A skate park is a purpose-built space with ramps, rails, bowls, and ledges designed specifically for skateboarding, in-line skating, and BMX biking. Unlike skating on sidewalks or in parking lots, a skate park gives riders a controlled environment where they can practice tricks without worrying about traffic or angry store owners.

Building a skate park is not as simple as pouring a slab of concrete. City planners usually consult with local skateboarders before construction begins, asking which features riders actually want, such as a deep bowl for advanced skaters or a beginner-friendly area with gentle slopes. This kind of consultation has become common practice because early skate parks built without rider input often ended up unused, since the features did not match how people actually skated.

Safety remains one of the most debated topics surrounding skate parks. Studies of skateboarding injuries consistently show that wrist fractures and head injuries make up a large share of emergency room visits, and most of these injuries happen to beginners rather than experienced riders. Helmets, wrist guards, and knee pads dramatically reduce the severity of injuries, yet many teens skip this gear because they find it uncomfortable or believe it looks unfashionable among their peers.

Some skate parks have tried to encourage safety without seeming preachy about it. A few parks post friendly reminder signs at the entrance rather than strict rules, while others hold free clinics where local skate shop staff teach proper falling techniques alongside basic tricks. Falling correctly, by rolling instead of bracing with an outstretched arm, is one of the simplest ways to avoid a broken wrist, but it is a skill that takes deliberate practice to learn.

Beyond the physical benefits of exercise, skate parks often become informal social hubs. Teens who might not connect through school sports find a sense of belonging at the skate park, where skill level matters less than persistence and a willingness to keep trying after a fall. Many riders describe the atmosphere as more welcoming than competitive team sports, since skaters commonly cheer each other on even when they are strangers.

City officials continue to weigh the costs of maintaining these spaces against their popularity. A skate park requires regular inspection for cracked concrete and loose hardware, and vandalism or graffiti can add unexpected repair costs. Even so, most cities that have built skate parks report that ridership stays high year after year, suggesting the investment continues to pay off for the community.
