Passage information_paragraph_306

Many Ontario high schools have introduced recycling and composting programs over the past decade, aiming to reduce the amount of garbage that ends up in landfills. A typical program places three separate bins in cafeterias and hallways: one for garbage, one for recyclables like bottles and cans, and one for organic waste such as food scraps and compostable packaging. Students and staff are expected to sort their own waste before throwing it away, though the system only works well when everyone understands which bin each item belongs in.

Getting a school-wide program to actually function smoothly is harder than it sounds. Custodial staff at several schools have reported that recycling and garbage bins often end up mixed together because students are unsure which bin to use or simply choose the closest one without checking the labels. To address this confusion, some schools have added clear pictures above each bin showing common items, along with student volunteers who stand nearby during lunch to answer questions and guide sorting.

Composting in particular requires extra care because contaminated organic waste, meaning food scraps mixed with plastic wrappers, cannot be processed properly at composting facilities. A single sandwich bag tossed into the compost bin can force an entire batch to be rejected and sent to a landfill instead, undoing the effort of everyone who sorted correctly. For this reason, schools with successful composting programs usually invest time in teaching students exactly which packaging is compostable and which only looks like it is.

The environmental payoff of these programs can be significant when they work as intended. Diverting food waste from landfills reduces the production of methane, a greenhouse gas released as organic material breaks down without oxygen in a landfill. Schools that track their waste diversion rates have found that a well-run program can cut the amount of garbage sent to landfill by nearly half, a number that surprises many students who assumed most of their lunch waste could not be reused in any way.

Some schools have gone further by starting their own composting sites on school property, using the finished compost in a vegetable garden or donating it to a community garden nearby. Student environmental clubs often lead these projects, applying for small grants to purchase composting bins and organizing awareness campaigns during lunch periods. Teachers involved in these clubs note that students who help run the program tend to become far more careful about their own sorting habits, since they have seen firsthand how much effort goes into managing waste properly.

Despite these successes, recycling and composting programs still depend heavily on continued participation. A program can run smoothly for months and then fall apart quickly if student volunteers graduate and nobody replaces them, or if a school stops replacing worn-out signage. Sustaining these programs long term appears to require more than good intentions: it requires ongoing education, visible reminders, and people willing to keep explaining the system to each new group of students.

Passage information_paragraph_312

Public libraries have changed dramatically over the past twenty years, moving well beyond their traditional role as quiet rooms full of books. Many branches across Ontario now include what librarians call a makerspace, a section of the library equipped with tools such as 3D printers, sewing machines, sound recording equipment, and sometimes even laser cutters. Visitors, including teenagers with a library card, can book time on this equipment for free or for a small material fee, giving them access to technology that would otherwise be far too expensive to own personally.

The shift toward makerspaces reflects a broader change in how librarians think about their mission. Rather than simply lending out finished books, many libraries now see themselves as places that help people create things, whether that means designing a phone case on a 3D printer, recording a podcast episode, or learning to code a simple video game. Library staff say this approach reaches teenagers who might never have visited the library otherwise, since a maker project gives them a concrete reason to walk through the doors.

Learning to use unfamiliar equipment safely is a central part of any makerspace program. Before a first-time visitor can use a 3D printer or sewing machine, most libraries require a short orientation session covering basic operation and safety rules. Staff members describe these sessions as essential, since even simple machines can cause injury or produce ruined materials if used incorrectly, and a five-minute lesson often prevents hours of frustration later.

Makerspaces have also become a place where teenagers develop skills that extend well beyond the specific project they are working on. A student who struggles through several failed 3D prints before getting one to work is practicing patience and problem-solving, even if they came in only hoping to make a small toy. Some libraries have noticed that regular makerspace visitors return with more ambitious projects over time, suggesting that early frustration eventually builds real confidence.

Funding remains an ongoing challenge for libraries trying to maintain this equipment. A 3D printer needs replacement filament, and expensive machines occasionally break down and require costly repairs that a small branch may not be able to afford right away. Some libraries have partnered with local businesses or applied for government grants specifically meant to support digital literacy programs, since without this outside funding many makerspaces would struggle to stay stocked and running.

Despite the costs, most librarians argue that makerspaces are worth the investment because they change how young people see the library itself. A teenager who once thought of the library only as a place for silent studying may leave with a completed project, a new skill, and a reason to come back. For many library systems, that shift in perception has become just as valuable as the books still sitting on the shelves.

Passage information_paragraph_318

For many Ontario teenagers, a part-time job marks one of the first real steps toward financial independence. Common first jobs include working at a grocery store, a fast-food restaurant, a summer camp, or a retail shop at the local mall. While the pay for these positions is often close to minimum wage, many teens say the value of the job goes beyond the paycheque itself, since it introduces them to responsibilities they rarely encounter in a classroom setting.

Balancing schoolwork with a part-time job is one of the biggest challenges teenage employees face. Guidance counsellors frequently advise students to limit their work hours during exam periods, since research consistently shows that grades can suffer when students work more than fifteen to twenty hours per week during the school year. Employers who regularly hire students, particularly those in retail and food service, have become more accustomed to accommodating school schedules, though conflicts over shift scheduling still arise, especially around exam time.

A first job also teaches skills that are difficult to learn any other way. Handling a difficult customer, working as part of a team during a busy rush, and learning to show up on time even when tired all require practice that no textbook can fully replace. Employers who hire teenagers often note that the ones who succeed tend to be reliable rather than simply talented, since showing up consistently and following through on commitments matters more in most entry-level jobs than natural ability.

Money management becomes a real issue for many teens the moment their first paycheque arrives. Some open a savings account for the first time, learning how interest works or setting aside money for a future goal like a car or post-secondary tuition. Others struggle to resist spending everything quickly on clothes, food, or entertainment, only realizing months later how little of their earnings they actually saved. Financial educators suggest that a first job is one of the best opportunities for teens to practice budgeting while the financial stakes are still relatively low.

Workplace safety is another area where young workers sometimes lack experience. Ontario's employment standards include specific protections for workers under eighteen, including restrictions on the type of work minors can perform and rules limiting how late they can be scheduled on a school night. Despite these protections, workplace injuries among young employees still happen more often than among older, more experienced staff, frequently because a new worker was never properly shown how to use a piece of equipment safely.

For all its challenges, most teens who have held a part-time job describe it as a valuable experience overall. Many say it changed how they see the value of money, made them more comfortable talking to strangers, and gave them a reference for future job applications. Even teens who found their first job stressful at times often admit, looking back, that the experience taught them something a classroom alone could not.

Passage information_paragraph_324

In the last fifteen years, keeping honeybee hives on city rooftops has grown from an unusual hobby into a recognized part of urban agriculture in many Canadian cities. Rooftop apiaries, meaning collections of managed beehives, now sit above hotels, office buildings, and even some high schools, producing honey that is sold locally or used in school cooking programs. Supporters of urban beekeeping argue that cities actually offer bees a wider variety of flowering plants than rural areas dominated by a single crop, since gardens, parks, and boulevard trees bloom at different times throughout the season.

Bees play a role in the food system that most people rarely think about until it is explained to them. Many fruits and vegetables, including apples, blueberries, and squash, depend on pollinators to transfer pollen between flowers before they can produce fruit. Without enough bees visiting these plants, yields can drop noticeably, which is why some farms rent hives from beekeepers during the weeks their crops are in bloom rather than relying only on wild pollinators.

Concerns about declining bee populations have made this topic more urgent in recent years. Beekeepers have reported unusually high losses of entire hives over the winter months, a problem linked to several combined causes, including pesticide exposure, loss of wildflower habitat, and a parasitic mite that weakens colonies. While a single rooftop hive in a city will not reverse this broader trend on its own, beekeeping advocates argue that urban hives raise public awareness and encourage residents to plant more pollinator-friendly gardens.

Managing a hive safely in a crowded city setting requires careful planning that rural beekeepers rarely need to consider. Hives are usually placed on rooftops specifically to keep bees away from pedestrians at street level, and beekeepers must think carefully about flight paths so that bees leaving the hive do not cross directly over a busy sidewalk or an open window. Most cities that allow beekeeping also require hives to be registered, and some set limits on how many hives a single property can host.

Schools that maintain their own rooftop or courtyard hives often build a course unit around them, teaching students about pollination, hive structure, and the surprisingly complex communication system bees use to share information about nearby flowers. Students who help harvest honey in the fall often describe it as one of the more memorable parts of the school year, partly because few of them had ever seen a real beehive up close before.

Not everyone is convinced urban beekeeping is the best use of city space, however. A small number of critics argue that domesticated honeybees can compete with wild native bee species for limited flowers, potentially harming the very biodiversity beekeeping advocates hope to support. This debate remains unresolved, and researchers continue to study how urban hives affect the balance of pollinator species living nearby.

Passage information_paragraph_330

Active transportation, a term used to describe any way of getting somewhere under your own power such as walking, cycling, or skateboarding, has become a focus for many Ontario school boards looking to reduce the number of cars dropping students off each morning. Streets near schools tend to become congested and occasionally dangerous during drop-off and pickup times, with parked cars blocking sightlines and drivers sometimes making quick, unsafe turns while trying to find a parking spot. Encouraging more students to walk or cycle is seen as one way to ease this daily traffic problem.

Cycling to school offers benefits that go beyond avoiding morning traffic. Regular physical activity before the school day begins has been linked to improved concentration in early morning classes, and some teachers report that students who arrive after a walk or bike ride seem more alert during first period than those who were driven directly from bed to the classroom door. For teens without easy access to a car or a ride, cycling can also provide a sense of independence that relying on a parent for transportation does not offer.

Safety concerns remain the most common reason parents hesitate to let their teens cycle to school, particularly on roads without dedicated bike lanes. Cities that have added protected bike lanes near schools, meaning lanes physically separated from car traffic by a curb or barrier, have generally seen cycling rates among students rise, since parents feel more confident their children are not sharing a lane directly with cars. Where no such infrastructure exists, some schools have organized bike trains instead, in which a group of students cycles together along a set route accompanied by a parent volunteer or staff member.

Weather and distance remain practical barriers that infrastructure alone cannot solve. A student who lives eight kilometres from school is unlikely to cycle there daily regardless of how safe the route is, and Ontario winters make cycling far less appealing for months at a time. Because of this, most active transportation programs focus on students who live within a reasonable distance, generally under three kilometres, and encourage cycling primarily during the milder months of the school year.

Bike storage at school is a smaller but still important piece of the puzzle. A school with only a handful of exposed bike racks near a busy entrance may discourage cycling simply because students worry about theft or vandalism, while covered, secure bike storage tends to see much higher use. Some schools have added bike repair stations with basic tools, allowing students to fix a loose chain or a flat tire without needing to walk the rest of the way.

Overall, school boards that have invested in active transportation programs report gradual rather than dramatic increases in cycling rates. Changing daily habits takes time, and a single new bike lane rarely transforms a neighbourhood's transportation patterns overnight. Even so, staff involved in these programs describe steady progress as worthwhile, arguing that every student who switches from being driven to cycling represents one less car in an already congested drop-off line.

Passage information_paragraph_336

Sleep researchers have long noted that teenagers naturally shift toward a later sleep schedule during adolescence, a biological change driven by shifts in the hormone melatonin rather than simple laziness or poor discipline. This shift means that many teens do not start feeling genuinely sleepy until closer to eleven at night, even if they need to wake up at six thirty the next morning for school. The mismatch between a teen's natural sleep rhythm and an early school start time has become a significant topic of discussion among educators and health professionals across Ontario.

The consequences of chronic sleep deprivation among teens extend well beyond simply feeling tired. Studies consistently link insufficient sleep to reduced concentration, slower reaction times, and a weaker ability to regulate emotions, meaning a sleep-deprived teen may become irritable or overwhelmed more easily than usual. Athletic performance also suffers, since muscle recovery and reaction speed both depend heavily on adequate rest, a fact that some student athletes overlook while focusing entirely on practice time.

Screens play a complicated role in this sleep story. The blue light emitted by phone and computer screens can suppress melatonin production, making it harder to fall asleep even after putting the device down. Beyond the light itself, an engaging conversation or an exciting video can also simply keep a teen mentally alert well past the point when they should be winding down for the night, regardless of the light involved. Sleep experts generally recommend setting screens aside at least thirty minutes before bedtime, though many teens admit this guideline is one of the hardest sleep habits to actually follow.

Some Ontario school boards have experimented with later start times in response to this research, moving the first bell from eight thirty to nine or later. Early results from these changes have generally shown modest improvements in attendance and reported alertness, though shifting a start time creates its own complications, including changes to after-school bus schedules, sports practices, and part-time work shifts that students had already arranged around the old timetable. Not every school board has been willing to make this change, partly because rearranging an entire transportation system is a significant undertaking.

Beyond school schedules, sleep experts point to daily habits that teens can control more directly. Keeping a consistent bedtime, even on weekends, helps regulate the body's internal clock more effectively than sleeping in dramatically on Saturday only to struggle falling asleep again on Sunday night. Caffeine consumed in the afternoon, whether from coffee, energy drinks, or even chocolate, can also linger in the body for hours and interfere with falling asleep later that night.

While no single change guarantees better sleep for every teenager, health professionals generally agree that small, consistent habits tend to matter more than occasional, dramatic efforts. A teen who goes to bed thirty minutes earlier every night is likely to see more benefit over time than one who tries to catch up on missed sleep with a single long nap on the weekend.

Option D is never mentioned anywhere in the passage.

Option A is never mentioned in the passage. Option B is also not supported anywhere. Option C directly contradicts the passage, which says attendance modestly improved.

Passage information_paragraph_342

Volunteering has become a common way for Ontario teenagers to get involved in their communities, and for many students it is also a graduation requirement. Since 1999, Ontario high school students have needed to complete forty hours of community involvement activities before receiving their diploma, a policy intended to encourage young people to contribute to causes beyond their own daily lives. While the requirement is mandatory, many students continue volunteering well past the forty-hour minimum once they find a cause that genuinely interests them.

The range of volunteer opportunities available to teens is wider than many students initially realize. Local food banks need help sorting donations, animal shelters rely on volunteers to walk dogs and clean kennels, and community events such as fun runs or festivals often depend entirely on volunteer labour to run smoothly. Some students choose opportunities related to a future career interest, such as volunteering at a hospital gift shop if they hope to study medicine, while others simply pick whatever cause feels personally meaningful to them.

Community events in particular depend on a surprising number of volunteer roles working together behind the scenes. A single neighbourhood festival might need volunteers to set up tents before the event, direct traffic in the parking area, staff an information booth, and take down equipment once everyone has gone home. Event organizers often say that the hardest roles to fill are not the most visible ones, like handing out programs, but the early morning setup and late evening cleanup shifts that most attendees never see.

Beyond satisfying a graduation requirement, volunteering offers benefits that students sometimes only notice after the fact. Talking to strangers at a booth, coordinating with other volunteers under time pressure, and following instructions from an unfamiliar supervisor all build communication skills that transfer directly to future part-time jobs and job interviews. Several university and college applications also ask about volunteer experience specifically, giving students who volunteered consistently an advantage when describing what they learned outside the classroom.

Not every volunteering experience feels rewarding in the moment. Students sometimes describe early shifts as boring or physically tiring, particularly tasks like folding programs for hours or standing outside in poor weather. Experienced volunteer coordinators generally advise new volunteers to try a few different types of roles before deciding volunteering is not for them, since the right fit often depends less on the cause itself and more on whether the specific task matches what a student actually enjoys doing.

Option A is described as an easier role to fill, not a hard one. Option B is also not identified as difficult to fill. Option D is never mentioned as a requirement in the passage.

Option A is never stated as a requirement. Option C directly contradicts the passage, which describes many different types of accepted volunteering. Option D also overstates a claim never made in the passage.

Passage news_report_347

Maplewood Robotics Team Heads to National Finals

By Priya Anand, Community News Reporter

MAPLEWOOD - A team of five Grade 10 students from Maplewood Secondary School earned a spot at the Canadian National Robotics Championship after their robot, nicknamed Ferris, took first place at last weekend's regional qualifier in Thunder Bay.

The team, called the Circuit Breakers, spent four months after school designing and building Ferris to complete a series of timed obstacle challenges, including sorting coloured blocks and climbing a small ramp. Out of eighteen teams at the regional event, Maplewood finished with the highest overall score, edging out a team from a much larger school in the final round.

"We rebuilt the arm mechanism three separate times before it finally worked the way we wanted," said team captain Malik Osei, age 15. "There were nights we thought about giving up, but our coach kept reminding us that engineers fail a lot before they succeed."

Coach and technology teacher Dana Fournier said the win reflects months of quiet, unglamorous problem-solving rather than a single lucky moment. "These students showed up every Tuesday and Thursday for four months," Fournier said. "What people see is the trophy, but what I saw was five kids learning to disagree respectfully and keep testing ideas until something worked."

The national championship will take place in Ottawa in six weeks, and the team is now fundraising to cover travel costs for its five members, one coach, and the equipment needed to transport Ferris safely. A bake sale held Tuesday raised just over four hundred dollars, and the school's parent council has pledged to match any additional donations up to one thousand dollars.

Team member Sana Okafor, who focused on the robot's colour-sensing code, said she hopes the trip inspires younger students at Maplewood to join the club. "When I started in Grade 9, I barely knew how to use a screwdriver," she said. "Now I'm about to compete nationally. If our team can do this, anyone at this school can."

Principal Devon Whitlock said the school plans to livestream the national competition in the gymnasium so other students can cheer the team on from home. The Circuit Breakers will spend the coming weeks fine-tuning Ferris's programming and practising the obstacle course they expect to see in Ottawa, based on rules released by the national organizing committee last week.

  1. The correct answer is B (They competed in Thunder Bay, and they named their robot Ferris): the passage states the team took first place at last weekend's regional qualifier in Thunder Bay with their robot Ferris. Ottawa is where the national championship will be held, not the regional qualifier. Maplewood is the name of the students' school and town, not the competition site. The robot's name was Ferris, not Circuit, which is the name of the team itself.
  2. Nothing in the passage suggests she is downplaying talent to spare other schools' feelings. The passage never mentions equipment quality as a factor in the win. Her comment praises the students' effort rather than criticizing them for not trying hard enough.
  3. Winning a raffle involves chance, not effort or repetition. Receiving an expected gift involves no struggle or repeated attempts at all. Watching a recommended movie has nothing to do with practice or overcoming failure.
  4. The correct answer is B (By matching donations raised by the team up to one thousand dollars): the passage states the parent council pledged to match any additional donations up to one thousand dollars. The council did not agree to pay for the entire trip itself. The match is not limited to the exact bake sale total, which raised just over four hundred dollars. The pledge applies broadly to fundraising, not specifically to transporting the robot.
  5. The correct answer is C (That starting with little experience does not prevent later reaching a high level): Okafor notes she barely knew how to use a screwdriver in Grade 9 and is now competing nationally, directly suggesting beginners can succeed. Her comment contradicts, rather than supports, the idea that the club is only for experienced students. She does not suggest national competitions are too hard, since she encourages others to join. Her comment focuses on encouragement and growth, not on naming one specific technical skill as most important.
  6. A strong response should identify at least one specific challenge named in the passage, such as rebuilding the arm mechanism three times or learning to collaborate as a team, and should explain the team's response, such as meeting twice a week for four months. It should use specific details and names from the passage, like Malik Osei's or Dana Fournier's comments, rather than vague general statements. Example of a strong response: "The Circuit Breakers faced technical setbacks, including having to rebuild the robot's arm mechanism three separate times before it worked properly, as team captain Malik Osei explained. They also faced the challenge of learning to work well together, which coach Dana Fournier noted meant learning to disagree respectfully while testing ideas. The team met after school for four months, showing they responded to these difficulties through consistent practice rather than giving up. Their persistence paid off when they placed first among eighteen teams at the regional qualifier in Thunder Bay." New Skate Park Opens to Cheering Crowd at Riverside Park

By Jordan Achebe, Riverside Gazette

RIVERSIDE - Nearly three hundred residents gathered at Riverside Park on Saturday for the official opening of the town's first public skate park, a project that took almost two years to move from idea to concrete.

The park features a bowl section, a street-style area with rails and ledges, and a smaller beginner zone designed for younger skaters and first-timers. Town council approved the eighty-thousand-dollar project after a petition organized by local teenagers collected more than six hundred signatures two years ago.

"I started asking for this when I was thirteen," said Elena Marsh, now fifteen, who helped organize the original petition. "I never thought I'd actually get to skate here before I graduated high school, but here we are."

Mayor Terry Boudreau cut the ribbon alongside members of the youth committee that helped design the layout. "This park exists because young people in this town refused to stop asking for it," Boudreau told the crowd. "That persistence is exactly what we hope to see more of."

The design process included several public meetings where skaters of different skill levels suggested changes to the layout, including lowering one ramp that early plans had made too steep for beginners. Local skate shop owner Priya Chan, who volunteered to consult on the project, said the final design balances challenge and safety better than the first drafts did.

Not everyone supported the project at first. Some nearby residents raised concerns last year about noise and late-night use, and council responded by adding lighting that automatically shuts off at ten in the evening and posting clear hours on new signage at each entrance.

Marsh said she hopes the park encourages more girls to try skateboarding, noting that she was often the only girl at informal skate spots before this project began. "Having an actual park, not just a parking lot, makes it feel like this sport is for everyone, not just for one type of person," she said.

The town plans to host a beginner clinic next month, run by volunteer skaters, to help interested residents learn basic skills in a supervised setting before the park opens to unsupervised use in the evenings.

Picking up a hobby because a friend enjoys it does not involve years of advocacy or a long-awaited outcome.

"Students raised over eleven thousand dollars themselves through bake sales and a talent show," Delgado said. "That effort is part of why this space matters so much to this school."

By Sam Petrov, Northside Weekly

NORTHSIDE - This year's annual Northside Food Drive collected more than eighteen thousand pounds of food and household supplies, breaking the previous record by nearly four thousand pounds, organizers announced Monday.

"Without events like this, we would be turning families away right now," Delgado said. "This drive alone will stock our shelves for close to two months."

Organizers are already planning next year's drive and hope to expand the school competition to include elementary schools for the first time. Tran said the coalition will meet this summer to review what worked well and to set a new fundraising goal for next spring.

New Protected Bike Lane Divides Opinion on Elm Street

By Aisha Bello, City Desk

ELMWOOD - A new protected bike lane along Elm Street opened to cyclists this week, part of the city's plan to make routes near three local schools safer, but the change has drawn mixed reactions from residents and business owners.

The lane, separated from car traffic by a raised curb, runs for two kilometres past Elmwood Secondary School and connects to an existing trail near the river. City planners say the project responds directly to a safety study that found six reported cycling collisions along that stretch over the past three years.

"Students have been biking to school on that road for years without any real protection," said transportation planner Owen Reyes. "This lane gives them a physical barrier instead of just a painted line that drivers can drift across."

Grade 10 student Lily Marchetti, who bikes to school daily, said the new lane already feels different. "Before, I used to have a car pass close enough that I could hear the mirror," Marchetti said. "Now there's actually a curb between me and traffic, so I don't have to think about that every single day."

Not everyone is pleased. Several shop owners along Elm Street say the lane removed parking spots that customers relied on. "I've had regulars tell me they're driving somewhere else now because they can't find a spot," said Grace Kowalski, who owns a bakery on the street.

City council has agreed to review the lane's impact on local businesses after six months and will consider adjustments, including possibly adding short-term parking spots on a side street, if the data shows a clear drop in nearby sales.

Reyes said early counts show a forty percent increase in the number of cyclists using Elm Street since the lane opened, though he cautioned it is too soon to know whether that trend will continue once colder weather arrives.

  1. The correct answer is A (A study found six reported cycling collisions along that stretch over three years): the passage states the project responds directly to a safety study that found six reported cycling collisions along that stretch over the past three years. Student walking preferences are never mentioned in the passage. Highest car traffic citywide is not a claim made anywhere in the article. Removing parking to widen the road for buses is not part of the safety study described.
  2. The correct answer is C (They say the lane removed parking spots that customers used to rely on): the passage directly quotes bakery owner Grace Kowalski explaining that regulars now drive elsewhere because they cannot find parking. Delivery truck collision risk is never mentioned as a concern in the passage. The passage does not describe any complaint about a lack of consultation before construction. Attracting more cyclists is presented as a benefit of the project, not a complaint from shop owners.
  3. The correct answer is D (Feeling nervous walking near traffic and then noticing a safety barrier was added): both situations describe relief that comes from a physical barrier replacing an earlier feeling of being unsafely close to traffic. Choosing a shorter route is unrelated to safety concerns. Switching to walking due to weather has nothing to do with traffic protection. A compliment about a bike does not relate to safety from traffic at all.
  4. The correct answer is B (Review the lane's impact on local businesses and consider adjustments): the passage states council agreed to review the lane's impact on local businesses after six months and consider adjustments. The council did not commit to immediate removal based on a single complaint. Extending the lane further is not mentioned as a planned action. Replacing the curb with a painted line is never discussed anywhere in the passage.
  5. The correct answer is C (The lane may be encouraging more people to choose cycling, though it is early): Reyes reports the forty percent increase but cautions it is too soon to know whether the trend will continue once colder weather arrives, suggesting cautious optimism rather than certainty. The increase directly contradicts the idea that the lane failed to attract cyclists. The passage never claims the lane will solve all traffic problems. Reyes's own caution about colder weather implies the increase could be connected to the lane, not simply explained away by warm weather.
  6. A strong response should describe a specific benefit, such as improved cyclist safety or the forty percent increase in cyclists, and a specific concern, such as lost parking spots affecting business owners like Grace Kowalski, using details directly from the passage. Example of a strong response: "The new bike lane offers real safety benefits, giving cyclists like Lily Marchetti a physical curb barrier instead of just a painted line, which matters given the six reported collisions found in the city's safety study. The city has responded by agreeing to review the lane's impact on businesses after six months and consider adjustments like adding short-term parking on a side street." Example of a weaker response to avoid: "Some people like the bike lane and some people don't like it."

Underdog Soccer Team Stuns Rivals to Win Regional Title

By Marcus Lindqvist, Sports Desk

BRIDGEWATER - The Bridgewater United under-16 soccer team came from behind to defeat the heavily favoured Ashford Falcons three to two in Saturday's regional championship, capping a season that started with the team losing its first four games.

Bridgewater trailed two to nothing at halftime after a rough first forty minutes that included a missed penalty kick. Coach Ines Marchand said she made only one change during the break, moving forward Tomas Delacroix into a more central position.

"I told them nobody outside this room believed we could come back, and honestly some of us in the room barely believed it either," Marchand said. "But I also told them the second half hadn't happened yet."

Delacroix scored twice in the second half, including the game-winning goal in the final five minutes, off a pass from midfielder Priya Nair. Nair, who also scored Bridgewater's first goal, said the team's turnaround this season came from practising twice as often after their early losses.

"We were the team nobody expected anything from in September," Nair said. "Losing those first four games actually helped, because it forced us to figure out what we were doing wrong instead of pretending everything was fine."

Ashford coach Ryan Osei congratulated Bridgewater after the match, noting his team had not lost a game all season before Saturday. "Credit to them, they never stopped pressing even when we were up two goals," Osei said. "That kind of persistence is hard to coach."

The win qualifies Bridgewater United for the provincial tournament next month, the first time the club has advanced that far in over a decade. Team captain Delacroix said the group plans to keep the same training schedule that turned their season around, rather than change anything heading into the bigger competition.

Bridgewater was behind, not ahead, at halftime, which rules out the second option. The scores given for Ashford in the third option do not match the passage's actual numbers. The match was not tied at halftime; Bridgewater was clearly behind.

Winning easily contradicts the difficult start Bridgewater actually experienced. Immediate success with no setbacks is the opposite of what the passage describes. Giving up after early difficulty is the opposite of what the team actually did, since they increased their effort instead.
  4. Hiring a new assistant coach is never mentioned anywhere in the passage. The passage does not describe a permanent change to most players' positions. The passage does not suggest the team focused only on defence, since Nair and Delacroix are both credited with scoring goals.
  5. The new site, located two blocks away on land leased from a local church, will add thirty more plots and a small shared toolshed built partly with donated lumber. Volunteers spent three weekends this month clearing the lot and preparing the soil for planting.

Novak said the expansion was made possible partly by a small grant from a provincial environmental fund and partly by donations collected at last autumn's harvest festival, which raised enough to cover fencing and a water line for the new plots.

Families currently on the waitlist will be offered plots at the new site first, though Novak said he expects both locations to have full waitlists again within a year or two if interest keeps growing at its current pace.

Not all of the fundraising went smoothly. An early attempt to sell scoreboard-branded merchandise lost money after a printing error, forcing organizers to shift their strategy toward events instead of products partway through the year.

Voss said the association plans to use any leftover funds toward resurfacing the arena's parking lot next year, a project residents have requested for several seasons. The new scoreboard is expected to be officially used for the first time during next weekend's opening tournament game.

Local Teen's Photograph Wins Regional Youth Art Contest

By Grace Liu, Arts and Culture

BRANTLEY - A photograph taken by Grade 10 student Owen Marchetti has won first place in this year's regional youth art contest, beating out more than two hundred entries from schools across the region.

Marchetti's photograph, titled "Morning Shift," shows his older sister loading crates at the family's produce stand before sunrise. He took the photo last winter using a camera he borrowed from his school's media club after deciding his phone could not capture the low light well enough.

"I must have taken that same shot maybe thirty times over two different mornings before I got the lighting right," Marchetti said. "My sister was not thrilled about waking up early for photos she didn't even ask for."

Contest judge Priya Anand, a professional photographer who reviewed all entries, said Marchetti's photo stood out because of what it left unglamorous rather than what it dressed up. "So many entries showed dramatic landscapes or posed portraits," Anand said. "This photo showed ordinary work at an ordinary hour, and that honesty is rare in a youth contest."

Marchetti said he originally entered the contest on a dare from a friend and almost withdrew the photo after seeing more polished entries from other schools during the submission period. His media teacher, Renata Kowalski, convinced him to leave it in.

"I told him the judges were looking for something true, not something perfect," Kowalski said. "Sometimes students think a contest wants the fanciest image, when really it wants the most honest one."

The win includes a five-hundred-dollar prize and a spot in a regional gallery exhibit opening next month, where Marchetti's photograph will hang alongside other winning entries from four school districts. Marchetti said he plans to give some of the prize money to his sister for waking up so early.

  1. The correct answer is A (It is titled "Morning Shift," and it shows his sister loading crates before sunrise): the passage states the photograph, titled Morning Shift, shows Marchetti's older sister loading crates at the family's produce stand before sunrise. The photo is not titled Sunrise, and it does not show a landscape. It is not a self-portrait, since it shows his sister rather than himself. The photo was not titled Early Hours and does not show workers unloading trucks downtown.
  2. Feeling immediately confident is the opposite of Marchetti's actual reaction. A scheduling conflict is unrelated to the comparison-based doubt Marchetti experienced. Winning without any doubt contradicts the passage's clear description of Marchetti almost withdrawing the photo.
  4. The correct answer is C (He took it roughly thirty times across two separate mornings): Marchetti states he took the same shot maybe thirty times over two different mornings before getting the lighting right. Ten times over a single session does not match the passage's numbers or timeframe. Two attempts is far fewer than the roughly thirty described. Fifty times over one entire week also does not match the passage, which specifies two mornings, not a full week.
  5. The correct answer is A (Authenticity in a photograph can matter more than technical polish alone): Kowalski's comment, combined with Anand's praise for the photo's honesty over more polished entries, suggests genuine, true subject matter can outweigh a flawless technical finish. The passage never mentions editing software as a judging factor. Anand's actual comments favour ordinary, honest scenes over dramatic ones, the opposite of this option's claim. The passage focuses on honesty as the deciding factor, not visible effort specifically.

New Recycling Program Cuts School Waste Nearly in Half

By Tariq Hassan, School News Team

OAKRIDGE - A student-led recycling program at Oakridge Secondary School has cut the amount of garbage sent to landfill from the cafeteria by nearly half over the past three months, according to figures released by the school's environmental club.

The program, which sorts waste into compost, recycling, and true garbage bins at every cafeteria table, replaced a single garbage bin system that had been in place for years. Club members trained lunchroom staff and posted simple picture signs above each bin to guide students during the busy lunch period.

"People assume sorting garbage properly is complicated, but most of it is just habit," said club president Nadia Farouk. "Once students saw their friends doing it correctly, they mostly followed along without needing to be told."

The club measured results by weighing the school's garbage bags each week before and after the program started, a task that fell mostly to Grade 10 member Kwame Osei. "The first week felt discouraging because barely anything changed," Osei said. "By week six, our garbage weight had dropped so much I had to double check the scale wasn't broken."

Custodian staff supervisor Renee Girard said the change also reduced the number of overflowing garbage bins she had to deal with during the lunch rush. "Fewer overflowing bins means less mess in the hallway outside the cafeteria," Girard said. "That part alone made staff supportive of the change."

Not every part of the rollout went smoothly. Compost bins were removed twice in the first month after students mistakenly placed non-compostable trays inside them, contaminating the load. The club responded by adding clearer signage and assigning volunteers to monitor bins during the busiest lunch periods.

Farouk said the club plans to present its results to the school board next month in hopes that other schools in the district might adopt a similar system using Oakridge's data as a model.

  1. The correct answer is A (It reduced the garbage by nearly half over the past three months): the passage opens by stating the program cut cafeteria garbage sent to landfill by nearly half over the past three months. Ten percent within the first month is far smaller than the actual reduction described. The passage never claims garbage was eliminated completely at any point. A reduction of roughly a quarter understates the nearly half reduction the passage actually reports.
  2. The correct answer is B (Students who saw friends sorting correctly mostly followed along on their own): Farouk directly states that once students saw their friends doing it correctly, they mostly followed along without needing to be told. Mandatory training sessions are never mentioned in the passage. A cash reward for proper sorting is not described anywhere in the article. Custodial staff checking trays individually is not mentioned as part of the program.
  3. The correct answer is D (Expecting no real change from a new habit, then being surprised by real progress): Osei felt discouraged in week one when little had changed, then was surprised by dramatic progress later, matching a pattern of low expectations followed by real results. A routine with results that never change contradicts the clear improvement Osei describes. Giving up after the first attempt is the opposite of what the club actually did, since they continued the program. Achieving success immediately does not match Osei's description of slow, gradual progress.
  4. The correct answer is C (The bins were removed twice after students placed non-compostable trays inside them): the passage states compost bins were removed twice in the first month after students mistakenly placed non-compostable trays inside them, contaminating the load. Overflowing due to too few bins is not the problem described in the passage. There is no mention of custodial staff damaging the bins. The passage does not describe any delay in delivering the bins to the school.
  5. The correct answer is B (That other schools in the district might adopt a similar sorting system): the passage states Farouk hopes other schools in the district might adopt a similar system using Oakridge's data as a model. Shutting down the program contradicts the club's clear success and Farouk's evident pride in the results. Replacing club members is never mentioned or implied anywhere in the passage. Stopping garbage tracking elsewhere contradicts the goal of using data to encourage other schools to adopt the program.
  6. A strong response should describe the specific system change, separate bins with signage, and should mention the compost contamination problem and how the club solved it by adding signage and volunteer monitors, using details directly from the passage. Example of a strong response: "Oakridge's environmental club achieved its results by replacing a single garbage bin system with separate compost, recycling, and garbage bins at every cafeteria table, then training staff and posting picture signs to guide students, as club president Nadia Farouk explained. Rosewood Collegiate Raises Record Funds for New Gym Equipment

By Priya Nathan

BRAMPTON, ON — Students at Rosewood Collegiate raised more than eight thousand dollars during a three-day fundraising drive last week, smashing the school's previous record and securing new equipment for the gymnasium before the winter sports season begins.

The event, called the Rosewood Rally, combined a bake sale, a three-on-three basketball tournament, and an online donation page shared by students across social media. Organizers had originally hoped to raise five thousand dollars to replace a set of worn volleyball nets and a cracked scoreboard. Instead, the final total reached eight thousand two hundred dollars by Friday afternoon.

"We knew people cared about the gym, but we never expected this," said Marcus Ellery, president of the student council, who helped plan the rally with a small committee of Grade 10 and 11 students. "Every class brought something different. The Grade 9s ran the bake sale, and the older students organized the basketball games."

The basketball tournament alone brought in nearly two thousand dollars in entry fees, with sixteen teams competing over two afternoons in the school gym. Local businesses also contributed prizes, including gift cards and a donated pizza lunch for the winning team.

Physical education teacher Dana Whitfield, who has taught at Rosewood for eleven years, said the new funds will replace equipment that has been patched together for seasons. "Our nets have been repaired so many times that they barely stay up anymore," Whitfield said. "This means our teams can finally practice with equipment that actually works properly."

The school board confirmed that any funds raised through student events stay entirely within the school, meaning the full amount will go directly toward new volleyball nets, a replacement scoreboard, and several new basketballs for physical education classes.

Ellery said the student council is already discussing whether to make the Rosewood Rally an annual tradition, possibly expanding it to include a talent show next year. "People got really into it this time," he said. "I think if we do it again, we could raise even more."

The new equipment is expected to arrive before the winter volleyball season starts in early December.

Option B incorrectly assigns the bake sale to Grade 11 students, who actually helped with basketball. Option C ignores that specific classes, not just the student council, ran the event. Option D is unsupported, since local businesses only contributed prizes, not staffing.

City Opens Long-Awaited Skate Park Near Two High Schools

By Jordan Alavi

MILTON, ON — After nearly three years of planning, the city officially opened its newest skate park on Saturday, giving skateboarders and BMX riders near Milton Heights Secondary and Fairview High a dedicated space to practise for the first time.

The park, located on a former industrial lot beside Confederation Park, includes a street course with rails and ledges, a bowl section for more advanced riders, and a beginner area separated by a low fence. City officials estimate the project cost just under six hundred thousand dollars, funded partly through a provincial recreation grant and partly through the city's own budget.

More than two hundred people showed up for the opening ceremony, including several riders who had been asking the city council for a skate park since middle school. "I used to skate in an empty parking lot behind the mall," said sixteen-year-old Milton Heights student Owen Petrasek, who attended the ceremony with three friends. "Having somewhere actually built for this feels like we finally matter to the city."

City councillor Renata Sousa, who championed the project after students petitioned council in 2023, said the park was designed with input from local riders rather than being planned entirely by adults. "We brought in teenagers to review three different design drafts," Sousa said. "The bowl section exists because kids specifically asked for it during our second round of consultations."

Not everyone was pleased with the location. A few nearby residents raised concerns during city meetings about noise carrying into the surrounding neighbourhood in the evenings. In response, the city agreed to close the park at nine each night and installed sound-dampening fencing along the northern edge closest to homes.

Fairview High student Deja Okonkwo, who plans to use the park after school most days, said she hopes it will give younger kids somewhere safer to learn than the street. "A lot of us used to skate near traffic because there was nowhere else," Okonkwo said. "Now there's actually a real place to fall down safely."

The city says it will review how the park is used over the next year before deciding whether to add lighting for after-dark sessions.

Option D is false, since nothing in the article mentions charging admission at the ceremony.

Option A ignores that the passage credits teenagers, not outside designers, with shaping the design. Option C is not mentioned anywhere in the article. Option D contradicts the passage, since students were consulted directly rather than through the school board.
  4. Option A contradicts the article's focus on giving riders a safer place to practise. Option B is not supported anywhere in the passage. Option D is contradicted by the article, which describes some residents still raising noise concerns.
  5. Local Teen's Water Filter Invention Wins Regional Science Fair

By Sam Whitcombe

OAKVILLE, ON — A Grade 10 student from Oakridge Secondary took first place at the Halton Regional Science Fair last weekend with a low-cost water filter built almost entirely from recycled materials.

Priya Deol, fifteen, spent four months designing and testing a filter made from sand, charcoal, and plastic bottles collected from her school's recycling bins. Her project, titled "Clean Water for Less," aimed to show that effective water filtration does not require expensive equipment.

The judges awarded Deol first place in the environmental science category and named her project the fair's overall grand prize winner, beating out more than one hundred and twenty other entries from schools across the region.

"I wanted to build something that could actually help people, not just something that looked good on a poster board," Deol said after the awards ceremony. "My grandmother grew up somewhere without reliable clean water, and that stuck with me."

Deol tested her filter by running samples of muddy pond water through it and measuring how clear the water became compared to untreated samples. According to her data, the filter removed more than ninety percent of visible sediment after three passes through the layered materials.

Science teacher Faisal Rahim, who supervised the project, said Deol redesigned her filter four separate times after early tests failed to produce clear results. "Most students give up after the second failed attempt," Rahim said. "She kept adjusting the layer thickness until the numbers actually worked."

The win qualifies Deol to compete at the provincial science fair in the spring, where she will present alongside students from across Ontario. She said she plans to keep improving the design before then, including testing whether adding a layer of gravel improves filtration speed.

Fair organizers said Deol's project stood out for combining a real-world problem with materials any student could access. "It wasn't flashy," said fair coordinator Louise Tam. "It was just genuinely useful, and the data backed it up."

Deol said she hopes to eventually study environmental engineering in university.

Option C incorrectly claims the materials were purchased rather than recycled from the school.

Option A overstates the result, claiming complete removal after only one pass. Option B contradicts the entire purpose of the project, since the filter clearly improved water clarity. Option D invents a fifty percent figure never mentioned in the article.
  4. The correct answer is A (A simple, low-cost idea can still solve a real problem effectively): The article highlights how Deol's project used ordinary recycled materials rather than expensive equipment, yet still achieved measurable, useful results. Option B directly contradicts the article's emphasis on low-cost materials. Option C is a broad claim never made about science fair projects in general. Option D understates Deol's own role, since she made the design adjustments herself with only supervision from her teacher.
  5. The correct answer is D (Deol's comment that her grandmother's experience without clean water inspired her project): Deol explains that her grandmother grew up somewhere without reliable clean water and that this personal connection motivated her project idea.   6. Example of a strong response: "Deol spent four months testing and improving her water filter, which was built from sand, charcoal, and recycled plastic bottles. Westview High Cafeteria Introduces New Menu After Student Survey

By Aiden Cho

THUNDER BAY, ON — Westview Secondary School's cafeteria unveiled a redesigned lunch menu this week after nearly six hundred students responded to a survey asking what changes they wanted to see.

The survey, distributed by the student council last spring, asked students to rate existing cafeteria options and suggest new items. Results showed that more than seventy percent of respondents wanted more vegetarian choices, while over half asked for smaller, cheaper portion options for students on a tighter budget.

Cafeteria manager Teresa Bonelli said her team spent the summer testing recipes based on the feedback. The new menu now includes a daily vegetarian option, a half-size lunch combo priced two dollars lower than a full meal, and a build-your-own salad bar added three days a week.

"Students told us exactly what they wanted, so we listened," Bonelli said. "The half-size option came directly from comments saying some students couldn't afford full lunches every day."

Grade 11 student council member Tavita Faleolo, who helped design the survey, said the response rate surprised organizers. "We expected maybe two hundred replies," Faleolo said. "Getting almost six hundred showed us how much people actually cared about lunch."

Not every suggestion made it onto the menu. Several students requested a food truck parked outside during lunch, but the school board rejected the idea due to safety and liability concerns. Bonelli said the cafeteria is instead considering a monthly special-event lunch to make up for it.

Early reaction from students has been mostly positive. Grade 9 student Colby Reinholt said he has already tried the new vegetarian bowl twice. "It's actually good, and it's not just lettuce," Reinholt said. "I didn't think I'd want to eat it, but I keep going back."

Bonelli added that the cafeteria team is also exploring a rotating international menu day, inspired by requests from students who wanted more variety beyond the usual weekly rotation.

The school board said it plans to send out a follow-up survey in the spring to see whether the changes should continue into next year.

Maple Ridge Students Collect Record Number of Food Bank Donations

By Naomi Fitzgerald

SUDBURY, ON — Students at Maple Ridge Secondary collected more than four thousand items of food during the school's annual winter food drive, setting a new record and doubling last year's total.

The two-week drive, organized by the school's leadership class, challenged homerooms to compete against each other for the most donations. The winning homeroom received a pizza party, while the overall total was donated to the Sudbury Community Food Bank ahead of the holiday season.

Leadership teacher Ronald Achebe said the class set an ambitious goal this year after donations had fallen for two years in a row. "We told students flat out that donations were dropping, and that motivated them," Achebe said. "Once one homeroom pulled ahead, everyone else wanted to catch up."

The final count reached four thousand one hundred and twelve items, well past the class's original goal of three thousand. Grade 12 student Malia Osei, who led the leadership class's food drive committee, said social media played a bigger role than in past years.

"We posted daily updates showing which homeroom was winning," Osei said. "People started bringing in extra cans just to help their class move up the list."

Sudbury Community Food Bank director Wendell Kroeker said the timing of the donation could not have come at a better moment, since the food bank typically sees demand rise sharply in December. "Every can matters, but four thousand of them arriving at once makes a real difference to how many families we can help this month," Kroeker said.

The food bank has confirmed that donated items typically stay on shelves for less than a week before being distributed to local families, meaning the Maple Ridge donations will reach the community almost immediately.

Achebe said the leadership class is already planning to set an even higher goal next year, possibly extending the drive to three weeks instead of two. Osei said she hopes future students will keep building on what this year's class started.

"We didn't think we'd double last year's number," Osei said. "Now the goal is just to keep going up."

Passage news_report_437

New Bike Lane Connects Neighbourhood to Northfield Secondary School

By Diego Marchetti

KITCHENER, ON — A new protected bike lane connecting the Fernwood neighbourhood to Northfield Secondary School opened to cyclists this week, giving students a safer route after years of complaints about traffic near the school.

The one-and-a-half kilometre lane runs along Fernwood Avenue and includes a physical barrier separating cyclists from car traffic, something the previous painted lane lacked. City transportation staff say the project was moved up the priority list after a series of near-miss incidents reported by parents over the past two years.

"We had families telling us they drove their kids to school specifically because the old route felt dangerous," said city transportation planner Heather Voss. "This project directly responds to that concern."

The lane cost the city roughly four hundred thousand dollars, funded through a combination of municipal infrastructure money and a provincial active transportation grant. Construction took most of the summer, wrapping up just before the new school year began.

Northfield student council co-president Ezra Lindqvist said he has already noticed more students biking to school since the lane opened. "Before, maybe ten kids biked regularly," Lindqvist said. "In the first week alone, I counted way more than that just from my own street."

Not all reaction has been positive. A small number of local business owners along Fernwood Avenue argued the new lane removed street parking spots they relied on for customers. The city says it added replacement parking two blocks away to offset the loss.

Voss said the city plans to monitor cycling numbers along the route over the next year to decide whether to extend the lane further toward the neighbouring elementary school. "If ridership stays high, this becomes the model for other routes near schools," she said.

Northfield's principal, Carmen Diallo, said the school is considering adding more secure bike parking near the main entrance to keep up with the demand created by the new lane.

  1. Public Library Extends Hours for Students During Exam Season

By Priya Nathan

GUELPH, ON — The Guelph Public Library announced this week that its downtown branch will stay open two hours later on weeknights throughout January, giving students extra quiet study space during exam season.

The extended hours, running until nine each weeknight instead of the usual seven o'clock closing, follow months of requests from local high school students who said the library often felt too crowded to find a seat during exam weeks.

Library branch manager Colette Fournier said staff noticed the demand firsthand. "By four in the afternoon during exam weeks, every table upstairs was full," Fournier said. "Students were sitting on the floor between shelves just to find a quiet spot."

The library surveyed students at three nearby high schools before making the change, finding that more than eighty percent supported extended hours even if it meant fewer staff available during those extra hours. In response, the library reorganized part of its second floor into a dedicated quiet study zone with additional outlets for laptops.

Grade 12 student Tobias Renner, who studies at the library most weekday evenings, said the change already feels different. "Before, I'd sometimes just go home because there wasn't anywhere to sit," Renner said. "Now I can actually plan to be here until closing without worrying I'll get kicked out early."

The extended hours are being funded through a temporary staffing budget approved by the city council specifically for the trial period. Fournier said the library will track how many students use the space during the extra hours before deciding whether to make the change permanent or expand it to other branches.

Some students suggested the library should also extend hours during final exams in June, not just January. Fournier said that request has already been passed along to the library board for consideration ahead of next year's budget planning.

For now, the extended January hours apply only to the downtown branch, though the library says it may test the idea at its two suburban branches if the trial succeeds.

  1. The correct answer is D (The library will stay open two hours later than its usual closing time): The article states the branch will stay open two hours later, running until nine each weeknight instead of the usual seven o'clock closing. Option A understates the change by half. Option B and option C both exaggerate the length of the extension, since the library still closes at a set time of nine.
  2. The correct answer is A (The library wanted to confirm that students actually supported the trade-off involved): The survey specifically asked whether students supported extended hours even knowing it meant fewer staff available, suggesting the library wanted to confirm students accepted that trade-off before proceeding. Option B is never mentioned as a legal requirement. Option C is unrelated, since the survey focused on hours, not book selection. Option D is contradicted by the earlier detail that staff had already noticed overcrowding firsthand.
  3. The correct answer is C (The library created a dedicated quiet study zone with additional laptop outlets): The passage states the library reorganized part of its second floor into a dedicated quiet study zone with additional outlets for laptops. Option A is not mentioned as a change made to the space. Option B is unsupported, since relocating the book collection is never described. Option D contradicts the whole purpose of the extended hours, which was to give students more access, not less.
  4. The correct answer is B (Responding to a clear community need can lead to a meaningful service change): The article shows the library responding directly to overcrowding and student requests by extending hours and creating a new study space. Option A directly contradicts the article, since student requests are exactly what drove the change. Option C is contradicted by students requesting the same change for June exams as well. Option D is contradicted by Tobias Renner's comment about relying on the library rather than home.
  5. The correct answer is D (Tobias Renner's comment about no longer needing to go home early to study): Tobias Renner's comment directly describes the relief of no longer having to go home early because there was nowhere to sit, which matches the experience of struggling to find study space. Option A explains funding logistics rather than a personal study experience. Option B is a factual detail about the survey's scope. Option C describes a future possibility rather than a personal connection to the current problem.
  6. A strong response should: describe the specific evidence that prompted the change, such as overcrowding during exam weeks and the survey results showing strong student support, mention the physical study zone change, and explain that the library will track usage during the trial before deciding on a permanent change, rather than giving only a general statement. Example of a strong response: "Before extending its hours, the library first noticed that students were struggling to find seats during exam weeks, with some sitting on the floor between shelves. Eastwood Robotics Team Heads to Provincial Championship After Regional Win

By Marcus Delgado

WINDSOR, ON — Eastwood Secondary School's robotics team is heading to the provincial championship after placing first at the regional competition held last weekend, the team's best result in its six-year history.

The team, known as the Iron Falcons, competed against eighteen other schools in a challenge that required robots to sort and stack coloured blocks within a strict two-minute time limit. Eastwood's robot completed the task with the highest accuracy of any team, sorting all twelve blocks correctly with twenty seconds to spare.

Team captain Isabelle Nakamura said the group spent nearly four months building and reprogramming their robot after an early prototype kept dropping blocks during testing. "We rebuilt the gripper arm three separate times," Nakamura said. "The version that finally worked used rubber padding we found at a hardware store."

Faculty advisor Trevor Adeyemi, who has coached the team for three years, said this year's group showed more persistence than any team he has supervised. "They failed constantly in October and November," Adeyemi said. "Most teams would have given up on the gripper design, but they just kept adjusting it."

The win qualifies the twelve-member team for the provincial championship in March, where they will compete against the top teams from every region in Ontario. Nakamura said the team plans to spend the next two months refining their robot's speed rather than redesigning it further.

Adeyemi credited the team's success partly to new equipment purchased last year using funds from a school-wide bottle drive, which allowed students to test designs more quickly instead of sharing a single set of tools. Nakamura said younger members of the team, several of whom joined this year with no prior robotics experience, contributed ideas that older students had not considered.

"Sometimes the person with the least experience asks the question that fixes everything," Nakamura said. The team will begin fundraising this month to cover travel costs for the provincial competition in Toronto.

Option D incorrectly describes the competition as a race rather than a sorting challenge.

Option A is never mentioned as the funding source. Option B confuses the hardware store, which only sold the rubber padding, with a full equipment donation. Option C is not supported anywhere in the passage.
  4. The correct answer is A (Persistence through repeated failure can eventually lead to a strong result): The article emphasizes how the team failed repeatedly through October and November but kept adjusting the gripper design until it finally worked. Option B is contradicted by Nakamura's comment that newer, less experienced members contributed useful ideas. Option C oversimplifies the story, since persistence and testing mattered as much as the equipment itself. Option D directly contradicts the months of failure described in the article.
  5. Students Paint Mural Celebrating Diversity at Clarkson Secondary School

By Fatima Siddiqui

MISSISSAUGA, ON — Students at Clarkson Secondary School unveiled a large mural celebrating the school's cultural diversity on Friday, the result of nearly two months of after-school painting sessions led by the school's art club.

The mural, which stretches across an entire wall near the main entrance, features symbols and images submitted by students representing more than twenty different cultural backgrounds, including traditional patterns, flags, and phrases written in several languages spoken by the student body.

Art club president Renata Kowalski said the project began after students noticed the entrance hallway felt plain compared to the diversity actually present in the school. "We have students from all over the world here," Kowalski said. "The wall just didn't reflect that at all before this."

More than sixty students contributed to the mural in some way, whether by submitting design ideas, helping mix paint colours, or spending afternoons on the scaffolding used to reach the higher sections. Art teacher Desmond Okafor, who supervised the project, said students were given creative control over most decisions.

"I mostly helped with logistics and safety," Okafor said. "The actual design choices, the colours, the layout, all of that came from the students themselves."

Principal Ingrid Halvorsen said the mural has already changed how visitors experience the school. "Parents coming in for meetings stop and look at it before they even get to the office," Halvorsen said. "It tells people something about who we are before anyone says a word."

Kowalski said the art club hopes to add to the mural next year, leaving space for new students to contribute symbols representing their own backgrounds as the school's population continues to change.

The mural was funded partly through a small grant from a regional arts council and partly through donated paint supplies from a local hardware store that has supported school projects in the past.

Okafor said he hopes the project inspires similar murals at other schools in the district, several of which have already reached out asking how Clarkson organized its funding and scheduling.

New Recycling Program at Sundale High Aims to Cut Cafeteria Waste

By Owen Bratcher

BARRIE, ON — Sundale High School launched a new cafeteria recycling and composting program this month after an environmental club audit found that most lunchtime waste was ending up in the garbage despite being recyclable or compostable.

The audit, conducted last spring by the school's environmental club, involved sorting through a week's worth of cafeteria garbage bags to measure exactly what students were throwing away. Club members found that nearly sixty percent of the waste could have been composted or recycled instead.

"We expected some amount of garbage, but sixty percent surprised even us," said environmental club leader Halima Yusuf, who led the audit alongside four other Grade 11 students. "People just weren't sure what went where, so everything went in one bin."

The new program replaces each single garbage bin in the cafeteria with a three-part station labelled clearly for garbage, recycling, and compost, along with simple pictures showing common lunch items and where they belong. Club members volunteer during lunch periods for the first month to help students sort correctly.

Vice-principal Colm Bradshaw said the school approved the program after seeing the audit results presented at a staff meeting. "The data made it an easy decision," Bradshaw said. "We weren't going to ignore numbers that clear."

Yusuf said early results have been encouraging, with a follow-up mini-audit after two weeks showing garbage volume had already dropped by roughly a third. She credited the volunteer sorting helpers as much as the new bins themselves.

"Having someone standing there answering questions makes a huge difference," Yusuf said. "People want to sort correctly, they just didn't always know how before."

The environmental club plans to conduct a full follow-up audit in the spring to measure whether the improvement holds once volunteers stop supervising the bins daily. Bradshaw said the school may expand the program to classrooms if the cafeteria results continue to hold up.

Option C directly contradicts the entire purpose of the audit, which was to find misplaced waste.

Option D overstates the situation, since the article implies a single bin existed, not zero bins.

Option B invents a locked bin feature never mentioned. Option C contradicts the article's description of a three-part, not combined, station. Option D describes a weighing feature that is not part of the program.
  4. The correct answer is B (Clear labelling and volunteer help can meaningfully change how people sort waste): The article shows that labelled stations combined with volunteer helpers led to a real drop in garbage volume within just two weeks. Option A is contradicted by the roughly one-third reduction described in the article. Option C is contradicted by Yusuf's comment that people want to sort correctly once they understand how. Option D is never compared or discussed in the passage.
  5.
