Riverside High Robotics Team Rockets to Regional Final

By Priya Nandan, Riverside Community Times

Riverside Secondary School's robotics club, the IronHawks, placed first among sixteen teams at the Eastern Ontario Robotics Regional on Saturday, earning a spot in the provincial championship next month. The team's robot, nicknamed Sparky, completed every scoring task in the final round without a single mechanical failure, a first for the six-year-old club.

The IronHawks are made up of twelve students in grades nine through eleven who spend roughly eight hours a week after school designing, building, and programming their robot. This year's competition asked teams to build a robot that could sort recycling materials by weight and shape within ninety seconds.

"We rebuilt the sorting arm four times before it finally worked the way we wanted," said team captain Malik Osei, a grade eleven student. "The hardest part wasn't the coding. It was getting the mechanical pieces to move fast enough without shaking the whole robot apart."

The team's coach, science teacher Ms. Devinder Kaur, said the win reflects months of quiet, unglamorous work. "Nobody sees the Tuesday nights when a wire keeps coming loose and nothing makes sense," she said. "They just kept showing up anyway."

Riverside's win qualifies the IronHawks for the provincial finals in Ottawa, where they will compete against thirty-two of the top teams in Ontario. The club is now fundraising to cover travel costs, since the school board only covers a portion of the trip.

The club began in 2020 with just four members meeting in a converted storage room, and has since grown into one of the school's most popular extracurriculars, with a waiting list for new members each September.

Grade nine member Talia Fenwick joined the club just three months ago and said she almost quit after her first practice. "I didn't know anything about circuits and I felt behind everyone else," she said. "But the older students taught me instead of getting annoyed, and now I actually look forward to Tuesdays."

The IronHawks' next challenge is refining Sparky's sorting speed before provincials begin on October 24. Ms. Kaur said the team plans to run practice rounds twice a week until then.

Options C and D describe unrelated situations involving teaching an already-skilled group or a scheduling problem, neither of which reflects Talia's described feelings.
  4. New Program Pairs High School Students With Local Employers

By Owen Marchetti, Bellview Gazette

The Bellview Chamber of Commerce launched a new program last week connecting high school students with part-time jobs at nearby businesses, aiming to give teens real work experience before graduation. Called Bridge to Work, the program has already placed nineteen students from Bellview Collegiate in positions ranging from retail to landscaping.

Under the program, students commit to working six to ten hours a week during the school year, while employers agree to provide flexible scheduling around exams and extracurricular commitments. Organizers say the goal is to teach practical skills like customer service and time management that are not always covered in the classroom.

"A lot of these students have never had a real boss before, or filled out an actual paycheque form," said program coordinator Renata Silva. "We want their first job to be a good one, where someone is patient enough to actually teach them."

Grade eleven student Devon Whitfield was placed at a hardware store downtown and said the adjustment took some getting used to. "The first week I kept saying sorry every time I made a small mistake," he said. "My manager told me mistakes are normal when you're learning, and that helped me relax."

Employers involved in the pilot say they see benefits too. Marisol Chen, who owns a bakery on Fourth Street, hired two students through the program and said they bring energy the shop needs during after-school rush hours. "They're nervous the first few shifts, but they catch on fast," she said. "It also feels good knowing we're helping someone get started."

The Chamber of Commerce plans to expand Bridge to Work to two other high schools in the district next year, pending funding from a provincial small business grant. Organizers hope to eventually place more than one hundred students across the region.

Some parents have raised questions about how the program balances work hours with schoolwork, prompting organizers to add a rule limiting shifts during exam weeks.

Options B and C describe unrelated feelings of relief or annoyance that are not supported by the passage.

Volunteers Restore Maple Street Skating Rink After Years of Disrepair

By Camille Duarte, Northview Post

The outdoor skating rink on Maple Street reopened to the public on Saturday after a group of neighbourhood volunteers spent three months repairing cracked boards, replacing worn netting, and levelling the ice surface. The rink had been closed since 2023 after the city said it lacked the budget for repairs.

More than forty volunteers, including students from Northview Secondary School, contributed roughly six hundred hours of labour over the fall, according to organizer Jozef Bartkowski. The project began after his daughter mentioned she missed skating with her friends after school.

"I figured if the city couldn't fix it, maybe we could," Bartkowski said. "I didn't expect this many people to show up, but once word got around, we had more helpers than tools some weekends."

Grade ten student Aaliyah Boone helped repaint the boards and said the work gave her a new appreciation for the rink. "I used to just show up and skate without thinking about how it got there," she said. "Now every time I see it, I remember sanding those boards in the cold."

The rebuilt rink includes a small shelter with benches, donated by a local hardware store, and new floodlights so skating can continue safely into the evening. Organizers say the improvements should extend the rink's usable season by several weeks compared with previous years.

City councillor Priya Thambirajah attended the reopening and praised the volunteer effort, though she acknowledged it highlighted a gap in municipal funding. "This rink should not have needed a citizen rescue," she said. "I'll be pushing council to include proper maintenance funding in next year's budget."

Neighbourhood families say the rink's return means more than just a place to skate. For many, it has become a weekly meeting spot where friendships form over shared cups of hot chocolate near the new shelter.

  1. The correct answer is A (The city said it lacked the budget for necessary repairs): the passage states directly that the rink closed in 2023 after the city said it lacked the budget for repairs. Options B, C, and D all describe plausible-sounding reasons that are never mentioned in the passage and are not supported by any detail given.
  2. Option A does not match her active involvement and continued connection to the rink. Options C and D describe unrelated feelings of frustration or surprise that the passage does not support.
  4.   5. Lunchtime Board Game Club Grows From Four Members to Forty

By Nadia Fenwick-Cho, Elmwood Chronicle

What started as four students playing a card game in a quiet corner of the Elmwood Secondary cafeteria last September has grown into one of the school's largest lunchtime clubs, with roughly forty regular members meeting three times a week. The Board Game Club now takes up an entire section of the library during its meetings.

Founder Casey Whitmore, a grade eleven student, said the club began almost by accident. "My friend brought a game to play while we ate lunch, and other people kept asking if they could join," Whitmore said. "We never planned for it to get this big."

The club's growth has created some logistical challenges. Librarian Mr. Farrukh Aziz, who supervises the meetings, said storage has become a real issue. "We started with one small box of donated games," he said. "Now we need three shelves, and students keep bringing more from home to share."

Grade nine student Ines Popescu joined the club in October after struggling to make friends earlier in the year. "I'm not really someone who talks to strangers easily," she said. "But when you're playing a game together, you don't have to think of something to say. The conversation just happens."

The club has also started hosting monthly tournaments, with winners receiving small prizes donated by a local game shop. Whitmore said the tournaments have helped attract students who might not otherwise have joined a club focused on quiet games.

Mr. Aziz said he has noticed a change in the library's atmosphere since the club started meeting there. "It used to be dead silent in here at lunch," he said. "Now there's laughter, there's some friendly arguing over rules, and honestly, I think it's made the library feel more like a place students actually want to be."

The club plans to enter a regional board game convention in the spring, its first time competing outside the school.

City Creates Teen Advisory Council to Help Shape Library Programs

By Marcus Delacroix, Fairhaven Weekly

The Fairhaven Public Library announced this week that it is forming a Teen Advisory Council made up of students aged thirteen to seventeen, giving young people a direct say in what programs and technology the library offers. Applications opened Monday, and organizers expect to select twelve members by the end of the month.

The council will meet twice a month to review proposals for new programs, from gaming nights to homework help sessions, and will also weigh in on how the library spends part of its technology budget. Library director Hana Okafor said the idea came from repeated feedback that teens felt overlooked in planning decisions.

"We kept hearing that teens either loved the library or felt like it wasn't really built with them in mind," Okafor said. "Instead of guessing what they want, we decided to just ask them directly and give them some real influence."

Grade ten student Elijah Vandermeer applied for the council after noticing the library's teen section rarely changed. "Every time I went in, it felt like the same setup from years ago," he said. "If I get picked, I want to push for more computers and maybe a space where people can actually talk without getting shushed."

Okafor said the council's first major decision will involve choosing new devices for the library's technology lending program, which allows patrons to borrow laptops and tablets for up to two weeks. The council will review options and recommend which devices best fit teen needs and budgets.

Some long-time library volunteers have welcomed the change, though a few expressed concern about balancing teen priorities with the needs of younger children and seniors who also use the space. Okafor said the council's recommendations will still go through the full library board before becoming final.

Applications for the council close at the end of the month, and selected members will begin meeting in the new year.

Weekend Rush Turns Local Bakery Into Teen Training Ground

By Simone Achterberg, Riverbend Observer

Riverbend's busiest weekend bakery, Golden Crust, has hired six high school students over the past year to help handle its Saturday morning rush, turning what began as a staffing fix into an informal mentorship program. Owner Felix Ranaldo said he never planned to run a training program, but the arrangement has worked out better than expected.

Golden Crust sees lines out the door most Saturday mornings, when customers line up for fresh bread and pastries before they sell out. Ranaldo said he first hired students two years ago simply because he could not keep up with demand alone.

"I needed extra hands fast, and a few students from the high school down the street applied," Ranaldo said. "I figured I'd teach them to work a register. It turned into a lot more than that."

Grade twelve student Yusra Haidari has worked at Golden Crust for eight months and said Ranaldo taught her far more than she expected. "He showed me how to shape dough, how to talk to customers when we're slammed, even how to handle a mistake without panicking," she said. "I didn't think a Saturday job would teach me that much."

Ranaldo said he tries to rotate students through different tasks so they leave with more than register experience. "If someone only learns one thing here, that's a missed opportunity," he said. "I want them walking out knowing how a small business actually runs."

Not every shift goes smoothly. Grade eleven student Malachi Osei recalled a morning when the ovens fell behind schedule and the line kept growing. "I was sure we were going to run out of everything," he said. "But Felix stayed calm and just kept adjusting the plan, and somehow we got through it."

Ranaldo said he plans to keep hiring students each fall, partly because he sees it as a way to give back to a neighbourhood that has supported his bakery for over a decade.

Passage news_report_509

New Bike-Share Program Launches for Students Living Near Cedar Heights High

By Julian Restrepo, Cedar Heights Tribune

Cedar Heights High School introduced a student bike-share program this month, offering forty bicycles that students living within three kilometres of the school can borrow for the ride to and from class. The program aims to reduce reliance on car rides for students who live too far to comfortably walk but too close to qualify for the school bus.

Students can reserve a bike through a simple app the day before they need it, then pick it up from a locked rack near the school's main entrance. Bikes must be returned by the end of the school day, and each student is limited to two uses per week to keep enough bikes available for everyone.

Vice-principal Odalys Marin said the idea came after a survey found that many students in the "in-between zone," too close for the bus but too far for many parents to walk with them, were being driven to school every day. "We wanted an option that didn't depend on someone else's schedule," Marin said. "A bike gives students that independence."

Grade ten student Emerson Boyko was one of the first to use the program and said it changed his morning routine. "Before this, my mom had to leave work early some days just to drive me," he said. "Now I just grab a bike and go, and I actually feel more awake by the time I get to class."

Not everyone has adjusted easily. Grade nine student Priya Okonkwo said she was nervous the first time she rode in traffic near the school. "I hadn't really biked on busy roads before," she said. "The school gave us a short safety session, which helped, but I still go slower than everyone else."

The school has partnered with a local bike shop to handle repairs and maintenance, and plans to add ten more bikes in the spring if demand continues to grow. Marin said early feedback has been mostly positive, though some students have asked for more secure parking once bikes arrive at their own homes.

  1. Community Garden Turns Vacant Lot Into Neighbourhood Gathering Spot

By Harriet Solaris, Northgate Ledger

A vacant lot on Birchwood Avenue that had sat empty for nearly a decade has been transformed into a thriving community garden, thanks to a partnership between local residents and a group of Northgate Secondary School students. The garden officially opened to the public on Saturday, featuring thirty raised planting beds available to families across the neighbourhood.

The project began last spring when a group of students in an environmental science class proposed turning the unused lot into a garden as part of a class assignment. What started as a school project quickly grew into a full community effort once neighbours got involved.

"We honestly thought we'd just write a report and move on," said grade eleven student Owen Kaminski, one of the project's original organizers. "Then our teacher suggested we actually pitch it to the city, and somehow here we are with real dirt under our fingernails."

The city agreed to lease the lot to the community group for five years at no cost, provided the garden remained open to any resident who wanted to grow food there. Local hardware stores donated soil, lumber for the raised beds, and gardening tools to get the project started.

Neighbourhood resident Consuela Reyes, who has lived on Birchwood Avenue for over twenty years, said she remembers when the lot was cleared for a housing development that never happened. "For years it was just an eyesore full of weeds," she said. "Now I walk by and see kids and grandparents working in the dirt together. It feels like the neighbourhood again."

Teacher Ms. Odalys Ferreira, who supervised the students throughout the project, said she was proud of how far the idea had travelled from its classroom origins. "They did the research, they made the pitch, and they kept showing up even when the city process moved slower than they expected," she said. "This is what happens when you take students seriously."

Organizers plan to host a fall harvest festival at the garden in October, with proceeds from a small produce sale going toward expanding the garden next year.

Passage dialogue_521

Priya: Dev, have you signed up for the Spring Fundraiser committee yet? Mr. Alvarez needs volunteers by Friday.

Dev: I was thinking about it, but I can't decide between the bake sale and the walkathon. What are you doing?

Priya: I already signed up for the walkathon. It raised way more money last year, almost eight hundred dollars more than the bake sale.

Dev: That's true, but the bake sale only needs people to bring food and sell it at lunch. The walkathon means organizing a route, getting pledge sheets signed, and standing outside for three hours on a Saturday.

Priya: I don't mind the extra work. My cousin did a walkathon for his school last year and said it felt more like a real event, with music and a finish line banner. People got excited about it.

Dev: I get that, but not everyone can walk five kilometres. My little sister uses a wheelchair, and I don't think the route this year is fully accessible yet.

Priya: I hadn't thought about that. Did you bring it up with Mr. Alvarez?

Dev: Not yet. I wanted to check the map first before I said anything, because I didn't want to complain without a solution.

Priya: That's fair. Maybe there's a shorter loop near the school parking lot that avoids the gravel path by the ravine.

Dev: Actually, I already sketched one out last night. It's about two kilometres and stays on paved sidewalks the whole way.

Priya: Show it to me at lunch. If it works, we should bring it straight to Mr. Alvarez this week so there's time to change the pledge sheets.

Dev: Agreed. And honestly, once I know the route is fair for everyone, I'd rather help with the walkathon than the bake sale. Standing behind a table selling muffins for two hours doesn't really interest me.

Priya: See, I knew you'd come around. Between the two of us, we can probably get at least ten more people to sign up before Friday.

Dev: Let's split the list. I'll ask people in my math class, and you can ask the volleyball team.

Priya: Deal. And I'll mention your accessible route idea when I text the sign-up sheet around, so people know the walkathon is planned properly this year.

Dev: Thanks, Priya. I almost skipped signing up altogether because of the route problem, but now I actually want to help make it better.

  1. The correct answer is B (The route is not fully accessible for someone using a wheelchair): Dev explains that his sister uses a wheelchair and he does not think this year's route is fully accessible yet. He mentions a gravel path by the ravine as part of the concern. Option A is wrong because cost is never raised as an issue. Options C and D are not mentioned anywhere in the dialogue.
  2. The correct answer is D (He wanted to check the map and find a solution first): Dev says he did not want to complain without a solution, so he checked the map before saying anything to Mr. Alvarez. This shows he was already working on a fix. Option A is incorrect since Dev clearly remembers the issue on his own. Options B and C are never suggested in the conversation.
  3. The correct answer is A (A student notices a broken step and reports it along with an idea for a temporary fix): Dev notices the accessibility problem and works out a possible new route before mentioning it, similar to reporting an issue along with a fix. Passage dialogue_566

Ethan: Our student council meeting is about the proposal to start school an hour later next year. What do you think about it?

Priya: I'm all for it. Studies have shown that teenagers naturally stay up later and need more sleep in the morning than a typical school schedule allows.

Ethan: I get the science behind it, but if school starts later, it also ends later, which would cut into after-school practices and part-time job shifts.

Priya: That's a fair concern. My cousin's school made the switch last year, and some students said sports practices had to be shortened to avoid ending too late.

Ethan: If practices get shorter, coaches might worry it will hurt team performance heading into important games.

Priya: True, but students also said they were less exhausted during morning classes, which might actually help their grades and focus overall.

Ethan: I wonder if there's a way to test it first, like trying a later start time for just one semester before making it permanent.

Priya: That's actually a smart idea. It would let the school collect real feedback from students, teachers, and coaches before committing to a full change.

Ethan: We could bring that suggestion to the council instead of just voting yes or no on the original proposal.

Priya: Definitely. A trial period feels fairer to everyone, since it doesn't force a permanent decision based only on studies from other schools.

Ethan: I'll mention the idea of shortening the trial to just one semester when we present tomorrow, along with a short survey afterward.

Priya: Good plan. If most students and staff report feeling better about it, then the council can vote on making it permanent next year.

Ethan: And if it causes real problems with practices and jobs, at least it's easy to switch back before too much damage is done.

Priya: Exactly. It gives everyone a fair chance to see how it actually works instead of just guessing based on research alone.

This detail is stated directly in the dialogue. None of the other people mentioned in the options appear anywhere in the conversation. Passage dialogue_656

Zara: "Dad, the winter dance ends at eleven, but you always want me home by ten. Can we talk about maybe changing that just for this one night?"

Dad: "I hear you, but eleven is pretty late for a school night dance. What's the plan for getting home afterward?"

Zara: "Mia's older sister is driving a group of us, and she said she can drop me off right after the dance ends, so I'd probably be home around eleven fifteen."

Dad: "Who exactly is in this group? I want to know who's actually in the car before I agree to anything."

Zara: "It's me, Mia, Devon, and Priya. Mia's sister is twenty-two and has been driving us to things since last summer, so it's not a new arrangement."

Dad: "Okay, that helps. I still worry a little about a later night before a school day, though. What if we compromised on ten thirty instead of eleven fifteen?"

Zara: "That could work, but the dance doesn't actually end until eleven. Would it be okay if I called you the moment it's over so you know exactly when we're leaving?"

Dad: "That's a fair offer. If you call right when it ends and you're home by roughly eleven fifteen like you said, I think I can agree to that instead of the usual ten o'clock curfew."

Zara: "Thank you, that means a lot. I promise I'll text you as soon as we're in the car too, so you're not waiting and wondering."

Dad: "I appreciate that. Just remember, this is a one-time exception for the dance, not a permanent change to your regular curfew."

Zara: "I understand completely. I just didn't want to leave early and miss the last part of the night with my friends."

Dad: "That makes sense to me. I was your age once too, even if it doesn't always feel that way to you."

Zara: "I'll take your word for it. Thanks again for actually listening instead of just saying no right away."

  1. The correct answer is C (Eleven o'clock): Zara states directly that the winter dance ends at eleven, which is why she is asking her father to adjust her usual curfew for that one night. The other times listed are mentioned in the dialogue only as possible curfew compromises, not as the dance's actual end time. This detail is the starting point of the entire negotiation between Zara and her father.
  2. This shows his real concern is about safety, not about controlling Zara or looking for an excuse to refuse. He never suggests calling other parents, and he ultimately agrees to the later curfew once his safety concern is addressed. His question reflects reasonable parental concern rather than distrust.
  3. The correct answer is D (Offering a specific plan and a way to check in, while framing it as one-time): Zara explains exactly who is driving, offers to call the moment the dance ends, and treats the request as a one-time exception rather than a permanent change. This reflects a realistic and effective way to negotiate a rule change with a parent or guardian. The other options describe demanding, delaying, or avoiding the conversation, none of which match Zara's approach. Her clear plan and reassurance are what help her father feel comfortable agreeing.
  4. The correct answer is B (Twenty-two years old): Zara tells her father that Mia's sister is twenty-two and has been driving the group to things since last summer. This detail is stated directly in the dialogue and helps reassure her father about the safety of the ride home. None of the other ages listed match what Zara says. This detail plays a direct role in her father becoming more comfortable with the plan.
  5. The correct answer is C (They reach a reasonable compromise based on safety and communication): Zara's father agrees to a later curfew once he understands the driving arrangement and receives a promise of a phone call and text update. This shows a compromise built on addressing safety concerns and clear communication, not a flat refusal or an unquestioning agreement. Zara never considers skipping the dance, since her whole goal is to attend and stay until it ends. The conversation ends with both sides satisfied with the arrangement.
Passage dialogue_661

Camille: "Our class needs to raise three hundred dollars for the trip to the science centre, and we only have five weeks left. I think a bake sale is the easiest option."

Theo: "Bake sales are easy, sure, but they don't usually raise that much unless you run them for weeks. I was thinking a car wash instead. People pay more for that."

Reyansh: "What about a talent show? We could charge admission and sell snacks at the same time, so it's not just one source of income."

Camille: "A talent show sounds fun, but it also sounds like a lot of planning. Who's going to organize auditions, get a sound system, and book the gym in five weeks?"

Theo: "That's a fair concern. A car wash just needs buckets, soap, and a parking lot. Mr. Alavi already said we could use the staff lot on a Saturday."

Reyansh: "True, but car washes depend a lot on the weather. If it rains that Saturday, we'd lose the whole event with no backup plan."

Camille: "That's actually a really good point. We wouldn't have time to reschedule if the weather ruins our only planned date."

Theo: "What if we combined ideas instead of picking just one? A bake sale table at the car wash could bring in extra money from people just waiting around."

Reyansh: "That could work, and it solves my rain concern a little, since even if the car wash gets cancelled, the bake sale table could still happen indoors."

Camille: "I like that better than choosing only one option. Should we still consider the talent show for later in the year, maybe as a bigger spring event instead?"

Theo: "That makes sense. Trying to plan three events in five weeks would probably burn everyone out before the actual trip even happens."

Reyansh: "Agreed. Let's focus on the car wash and bake sale combo for now, and bring up the talent show idea to Ms. Okafor as a separate spring fundraiser."

Camille: "Sounds like a solid plan. I'll ask my mom if she can donate ingredients for baking, since she works at a bakery downtown."

Theo: "That would help a lot. I'll ask Mr. Alavi to confirm the parking lot booking and check if there's a backup indoor space in case of rain."

Reyansh: "And I'll make posters for both events so people around the school know the dates well in advance."

Camille: "Good, let's divide it exactly like that then. Between the three of us, I think we can actually pull this off in time."

Theo: "Agreed. I was worried we'd waste a whole meeting arguing about which single idea was best, but combining them turned out to be way easier."

Reyansh: "Same here. I think listening to each other's concerns is what actually got us to a plan that covers most of the risks."

  1. The correct answer is D (Three hundred dollars): Camille states directly at the beginning of the dialogue that the class needs to raise three hundred dollars for the trip to the science centre. The other amounts listed are not mentioned anywhere in the conversation. This detail establishes the goal that drives the rest of the discussion about fundraiser ideas. Recognizing this number helps frame why the students consider combining two fundraising ideas instead of relying on just one.
  2. The correct answer is D (Mr. Alavi, according to Theo): Theo mentions that Mr. Alavi already said the class could use the staff lot on a Saturday for the car wash. This detail is stated directly in the dialogue. None of the other people listed are mentioned as approving the parking lot. This detail makes the car wash idea seem more realistic and immediately doable within the five-week timeline.
  3. The correct answer is B (He wants the group to consider a backup plan in case of rain): Reyansh points out that car washes depend a lot on the weather and that rain could ruin the whole event with no backup plan, which leads the group to consider combining ideas. This shows his concern is about planning for risk, not about opposing fundraising in general or doubting the car wash's earning potential. Nothing in the dialogue mentions Ms. Okafor discussing an actual weather forecast. His concern is a practical, forward-thinking one.
  4. The correct answer is C (Asking her mom to donate baking ingredients): Camille says she will ask her mom, who works at a bakery downtown, if she can donate ingredients for baking. This task is stated directly and clearly divided among the three students near the end of the dialogue. The other tasks listed belong to Theo and Reyansh instead. This division of responsibilities shows the group organizing their plan efficiently.
  5. The correct answer is A (Combining the strongest parts of different ideas instead of insisting on only one option): Camille, Theo, and Reyansh each bring a different fundraiser idea, then realize combining the car wash and bake sale solves more problems than choosing only one. This reflects a useful real-world lesson about blending ideas rather than treating a discussion as a competition with only one winner. The other options describe favouritism, avoidance, or choosing the easiest path regardless of outcome, none of which match how the group actually resolves their disagreement. Their solution comes from genuinely listening to every concern raised.
  6. Nothing in the dialogue suggests Ms. Okafor has rejected the idea, that the students dislike it, or that it would not raise money, since Reyansh originally suggests it as a good moneymaking idea. Their reasoning is specifically about realistic time and effort within the five-week deadline.
  7. The correct answer is B (They feel confident because listening to each other led to a stronger plan): Theo says he was worried they would waste a whole meeting arguing, but combining ideas turned out to be easier, and Reyansh agrees that listening to each other's concerns led to a plan covering most of the risks. After enjoying the view for a while, they began the long walk back down before it got dark): This order follows the true chronological sequence of the hike, starting at the trailhead in the morning, moving through the climb and the rest stop, reaching the summit, and finally heading back down. Option A opens with the mid-climb rest stop before the hikers have even met at the trailhead, which reverses the actual timeline. Option B begins with the walk back down before any of the climbing has been described, confusing the sequence of events. Option C places the summit view before the meeting at the trailhead has happened at all.

Option D adds a stray comma in the middle of the dependent clause itself and still never completes the thought.
  30. The correct answer is C (you're): You're is the contraction of you are, which fits correctly before the verb finished in this sentence. You are've is not a real contraction and does not exist in standard English.
